Alcon · ALC-CH · the new Memo conveyor's card of 14 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Alcon

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 52,740 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “57th percentile” means the stock beats 57% of them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

D4633rd percentile

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces give Alcon a dependable but unexciting base and one acute, concentrated problem. The base is demographic cataract demand, a steady contact lens trade-up, a growing dry eye market and an oligopolistic structure. Together these support about 4% to 5% market growth, with Alcon's annuity lines (about 70% of sales) carried along mechanically. The problem is at the premium IOL edge: Johnson & Johnson's PureSee, Bausch + Lomb's enVista and RxSight's adjustable lens are capturing the accelerating premium conversion that the industry's own economics (falling surgeon fees) are creating. Alcon is losing share of the very trend that should have been its largest external tailwind. The 2026 tailwinds that flattered results (the tariff refund, a weaker dollar) are non-recurring or reversible, and 2027 adds input inflation, residual tariffs and payer pressure. At sector level, a de-rated and out-of-favour group makes external confirmation of any recovery slow. Model implication: treat external forces as neutral to mildly negative on revenue (no acceleration; organic growth of about 5% cc in 2027) and mildly negative on gross margin. Treat any premium share stabilisation as company-driven (Vivity Pro) rather than externally given.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0234C-0544C-0745C-0944C-3566C-3952
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3417th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising sharply at the single most profitable edge of the portfolio (premium IOLs in the United States), stable or easing in contact lenses, and broadly stable elsewhere. The intensification is current, has a stated duration of at least two more quarters, and is the main reason the company's revenue growth is decelerating.

  • Premium IOLs (the line that matters): Johnson & Johnson received United States approval for PureSee in March 2026 and is taking share from Vivity and PanOptix Pro, using free trials, aggressive sampling and a price about 15% (about $150) below Vivity in Europe. One surveyed surgeon intends to move 90% of extended-depth-of-focus (EDOF) cases to PureSee. Johnson & Johnson grew its United States surgical business 14% in Q3 (Redburn) against Alcon's implantables at -2%. Bausch + Lomb implantables grew 64% cc in Q2 2026 on the recovering enVista franchise (premium IOLs now 13% of its surgical sales). RxSight's light-adjustable lens is expected to rise from 10% to 12% of premium volume (Jefferies survey), and 41% of surveyed ophthalmologists expect Alcon to lose the most share to new entrants in 2027. The result is United States PC-IOL share at about 66% (from 73% to 74% in Q1 2026). Management says competitor sampling will "remain intense over the next two quarters".
  • Why it matters disproportionately: implantables are about 17% of sales but carry the highest margin in the surgical segment, and the market has chosen this line as its test of the whole case (shares fell 12% over two days after Q1 2026 despite an EPS beat; they fell after the Q2 beat-and-raise; and fell 4% after Q3).
  • Contact lenses: an oligopoly (top four about 90%) that the industry itself describes as "highly civilized"; Alcon reached a record share of about 26% while Johnson & Johnson keeps ceding share. The one new pressure is Bausch + Lomb's daily silicone hydrogels (+25% in Q3) and online and private-label price transparency outside the United States.
  • Equipment: Alcon holds more than 80% of the United States phaco installed base. Carl Zeiss Meditec's EVA NEXUS cataract configuration and its lead in digital workflow integration are a slow-burning challenge, but Zeiss is restructuring (ProfitUp, new chief executive) and weakened in China and the United States.
  • Elsewhere: Glaukos contracts aggressively in minimally invasive glaucoma surgery (MIGS); domestic Chinese makers win volume-based procurement (VBP) tenders.

Direction: intensifying through H1 2027; could ease from H2 2027 if Vivity Pro (January 2027) closes the optical gap, but there is no evidence yet that it will.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C-4451st percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the net effect was a tailwind in 2026 and turns into a mild headwind in 2027. The tailwind came from the tariff refund, manufacturing productivity and price; the 2027 headwind comes from refund non-recurrence, residual tariffs and new inflation in memory chips and resins. That inflation was serious enough for management to raise its restructuring savings target from $100 million to $130 million in November 2026 specifically to offset it.

  • Evidence that 2026 was a tailwind: core gross margin was 64.7% in Q2 2026 (+250bp). On FactSet's basis, which includes amortisation, gross margin was 59.3% in both Q2 and Q3 2026 against 53.5% and 55.8% a year earlier. The drivers were price, factory efficiency (DSM Flex lines, Singapore's $503 million expansion), a $15 million licensee windfall in Q2, and the refund in Q3.
  • The 2027 headwinds: (i) tariffs: gross $80 to $100 million a year with no refund, plus about $80 million of capitalised tariffs flowing through cost of goods with a lag; (ii) memory chips (relevant to Unity consoles and microscopes) and resins (contact lenses), which Bernstein names explicitly; (iii) the Swiss franc: Alcon's Swiss and United States costs exceed revenues in those currencies (Switzerland is 1% of sales), a 1% dollar move is worth $29 million of operating income, and the banks quoted forecast a firmer franc.
  • Supplier concentration: low by spend (the largest direct-material supplier is 5% of spend; about 90% of products are made in-house; buffer stocks target at least 12 weeks), but single-source dependencies remain in hyaluronic acid (Lifecore), sterilisation, viscoelastics and some active ingredients. Alcon is reducing the Lifecore dependency (Lifecore cut its 2026 revenue forecast because of Alcon's supply-chain changes), which de-risks supply but marks this as a live management topic. Precedents: a $30 million contaminated raw-material write-down in Q2 2024 (core gross margin -120bp per the Risks digest), and packaging shortages from 2022 to mid-2023.

For the model: hold core gross margin roughly flat to +50bp in 2027 (price, mix toward Ocular Health and the new contact lens lines, against tariffs, chip and resin inflation, and the lower-priced Unity CS mix), and take margin expansion mainly from operating expenses.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C+4568th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro bites hardest at the sector level, through the discount rate and risk appetite (the multiple); second at the company level, through premium out-of-pocket mix and ASC capital budgets; and least at the industry level, where procedure volumes are set by demographics and surgeon capacity, not the economy.

  • Sector (valuation): high government bond yields (United States 10-year near 5%, the Fed's direction "not established" per the 13-Nov-26 environment digest) and a risk-off regime beneath the index have pushed the medtech consumables sector to a median 17.1x, at the 2nd percentile of its own ten-year range. The sector's relative strength grade is F, and part of its recent three-month rise may be technology money parking in healthcare that "can leave again". Alcon's de-rating since May 2026, while its earnings were beaten and raised, is about half sector-driven (memo).
  • Company (earnings): consumer budget pressure (inflation, rising use of instalment plans) affects premium IOL conversion and elective timing; high rates and reimbursement cuts constrain ASC capital purchases, so the equipment cycle is exposed. Currency: a weaker dollar has flipped from a drag (more than 100bp of margin a year from 2022 to 2024; $0.15 of EPS in 2025) to a tailwind (+90bp on 2026 revenue, +160bp on core EPS growth). The structural franc cost mismatch means a sharp safe-haven franc rally would hit margins.
  • Industry (volumes): cataract surgery is non-deferrable over more than a few months, and contact lens wear is habitual; macro has little effect on volume.
  • Offsetting macro positive: the regime favours large, low-leverage, simple defensives (the Fragility row), which Alcon is, but only if the market accepts Alcon as one of the "clean" defensives, and at present it does not.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–44

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other dynamics matter. Two are negative (the sector's de-rating; customer consolidation shifting bargaining power), and one is a positive option that consensus does not model (easing of surgeon capacity).

  • Sector de-rating and rotation (negative, large, uncontrollable): eleven of seventeen sector peers sit at or below the 5th percentile of their own multiple history; Carl Zeiss Meditec de-rated from 37x to 16x over three years. Alcon's 10-Aug and 10-Nov beats were followed by lower share prices. The sector's own note ("a results beat alone does not lift a share price; watch for the first beat-and-raise the share price rewards") describes Alcon exactly.
  • Customer consolidation (negative, slow): private-equity-backed ASC and ophthalmology platforms shift purchasing authority from surgeons to administrators, who buy multi-vendor (for example, premium knives bought separately from packs) and demand return-on-investment proof before upgrading equipment. Optometrist buying groups and retail chains (Costco) push for discounts on contact lenses. EssilorLuxottica bought Optegra (hospital network), and Amplifon's purchase of GN Hearing shows how far downstream integration can go in an adjacent industry. All of this erodes the bundling power on which Alcon's surgical franchise was built.
  • Surgeon-capacity easing (positive option): if capacity normalises (staffing, operating-room hours, wider scope of practice), procedure volumes could accelerate sharply, disproportionately benefiting the owner of 80% of United States phaco consoles. Deutsche Bank calls this consensus's blind spot. It is undated; treat it as upside, not base case.
  • Ownership signal (mild positive): Pershing Square added Alcon during the de-rating and argues margins can reach 25% or more; European investors pay a scarcity premium for large-cap medtech growth.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B6682nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the new-entrant threat is low to moderate. The only material entrant is orthogonal (adjustable lenses), and Alcon has partly neutralised it by partnering.

  • Orthogonal or innovative: RxSight's light-adjustable lens is the real case, at about 10% of premium volume and rising, a change of paradigm (adjust after surgery) rather than a better static optic. The threat is moderate: RxSight's commercial execution has faltered, and Alcon's licence gives it a route into the same technology. Robotics (ForSight) and pharmacological alternatives are distant or failing.
  • Adjacent: EssilorLuxottica (downstream into hospitals through Optegra, plus Heidelberg diagnostics) and Zeiss in digital workflow; smart glasses and hearing-integrated eyewear are irrelevant to cataract and contact lenses. Apple-style consumer technology has no route into implants. The threat is low.
  • Standard entrants: Chinese domestic IOL and consumables makers (in China only, via VBP), contact lens private label and Asian contract makers (Pegavision expanding in Vietnam). The threat is low globally, moderate in China (5% to 6% of sales).
  • Barriers: regulatory approval of Class III implants, clinical evidence, surgeon training and the installed base, plus manufacturing know-how (silicone hydrogel at scale).
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C-5254th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a modest source of risk rather than a support. Alcon's vertical integration (about 90% made in-house, 16 to 17 plants) contains the risk, but specific single points of failure and new component inflation remain.

  • Support: low concentration by spend (the largest direct-material supplier is about 5%); more than 95% of relationships are long-standing; agreements allow alternative sourcing of hyaluronic acid; buffer stocks of at least 12 weeks.
  • Risk: (i) single or limited sources for sterilisation, viscoelastics, hyaluronic acid (Lifecore; Alcon made up 42% of Lifecore's revenue and lent it $142 million against a $150 million commitment; a material breach of the supply agreement triggers a default under Alcon's credit facility, an unusual coupling) and some active ingredients; (ii) memory chips (for consoles and microscopes) and resins are inflating; (iii) past quality failures at suppliers (Q2 2024 contaminated raw material, a $30 million write-down and a limited recall; 2022-23 packaging shortages in lens care).
  • Trend: Alcon is actively dual-sourcing and insourcing (Lifecore's 2026 revenue forecast was cut because of Alcon's supply changes), which lowers the Lifecore risk over 2026 to 2027 but may add transition cost.

For the model: no explicit supplier event, but a cost-inflation headwind of about 20 to 40bp of gross margin in 2027 before savings, and a 50 to 120bp one-quarter gross margin hit as the tail scenario (the 2024 precedent).

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0157C-0342C-0442C-0852C-3256C-3743C-4354
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5742nd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand is structurally sound and highly visible in volume, but it is growing more slowly than its history, its composition is moving toward premium where Alcon's capture is contested, and there is no cyclical upswing with two or more years of visibility to lean on. Over the last six months, against the prior 12 to 18 months, volume did not change, premium mix accelerated at industry level, and capital spending became more cautious.

  • Cataract volume (the base of about 45% of sales: consumables and implantables): ageing adds about 3% a year mechanically, and the over-60 cohort doubles by 2050. But global cataract procedure growth has settled at about 3% (2% to 3% per Redburn, 11-Nov-26) against a historical 4% to 5%, and United States volumes were flat in 2026. Companies and brokers attribute the softness to surgeon capacity (staff shortages; premium counselling can double consultation-to-sign-off time), not to weak patient demand. This is structural, so it is highly visible: model 2% to 4% global volume growth for 2027 and 2028, with the United States at 0% to 2%. The upside option (capacity easing through staffing or wider scope of practice) is real but undated and should not be in a base case.
  • Premium mix (the value driver): industry premium IOL penetration is accelerating. United States penetration rose about 200bp in H1 2026 against Alcon's historical 50 to 100bp a year; Europe reached 16.1% (+260bp); global penetration is 17%, up from 12% in 2022; 78% of surgeons expect to raise premium use. The driver is surgeons offsetting Medicare fee cuts (-11% in 2026, a further -4% proposed for 2027), which makes it structural. The cyclical drag is affordability: about 60% of Alcon revenue is exposed to out-of-pocket spending, patients cite cost as the first barrier, and some practices see patients choosing enhanced monofocals. The net effect is a structural tailwind to the industry's value per procedure, but in 2026 the incremental premium volume went disproportionately to Johnson & Johnson's PureSee and to RxSight's light-adjustable lens rather than to Alcon.
  • Contact lenses ($11 billion; about 27% of Alcon sales): resilient, recurring, growing 4% to 5% in value. Trading up to daily silicone hydrogels brings 2 to 3 times the revenue per wearer, and about 10% of wearers are new each year. Cooper's flat Q3 reflected United States distributor destocking (channel inventory), not end-demand. International pricing is softer (online channels, private label).
  • Ocular Health (dry eye, OTC drops; about 18% of sales): structurally strong. Prescription dry eye is growing at double digits across the industry (Tryptyr, Miebo +44%), and Systane preservative-free volumes doubled year on year in Q3 2026.
  • Surgical equipment (about 10% of sales): demand is an Alcon-specific replacement wave (30,000-unit installed base on a ten-year cycle; Constellation dates from 2008, Centurion from 2013) rather than end-market growth. It was pulled into 2025 and 2026 and is now decelerating (+11% cc in Q3 against +24% in H1). Ambulatory surgery centre (ASC) capital budgets are constrained by rates and reimbursement, so visibility on 2027 equipment is low.

For the model: an Alcon-weighted end-market growth of about 4% to 5%, made up of surgical markets at 3% to 4% in value, contact lenses at 4% to 5% and Ocular Health at high single digits. Alcon's 5% to 6% organic growth needs premium mix and pricing on top of that, not volume.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4218th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the disruption that is strengthening and acting against Alcon now is a design shift in premium optics, from diffractive multifocals (Alcon's strength, PanOptix) toward non-diffractive, low-dysphotopsia EDOF and adjustable lenses. Most other disruption forces are weak, being disproved, or have been absorbed by Alcon. On balance disruption acts mildly against the company for 12 to 18 months and is neutral after that.

  • Strengthening, against Alcon: non-diffractive EDOF. PureSee is described by surgeons as "aberration-free and dysphotopsia-free" for intermediate vision, which lets surgeons offer premium lenses to patients with imperfect maculas, a wider candidate pool than Vivity has won. Alcon's answer, Vivity Pro, arrives in January 2027 with no published clinical comparison yet.
  • Strengthening, but partly absorbed: post-operative adjustability (RxSight's light-adjustable lens). Survey share is rising, but RxSight itself withdrew its 2026 guidance and changed chief executive in August 2026, so commercial momentum is weaker than surveys suggest. Alcon's non-exclusive licence ($60 million upfront, up to $140 million in milestones, 30% royalty) turns the threat into an option, though the joint lens is years away.
  • Disproved, cutting both ways: accommodating IOLs. Alcon's own PowerVision programme failed ($402 million impairment, Q2 2026). No rival has a working accommodating lens either, so the cost is to Alcon's long-term optionality, not its current position.
  • Being disproved: pharmacological presbyopia drops (Vuity, LENZ, Yuvezzi) face provider resistance and weak patient persistence. Direct-to-consumer and online contact lens channels are a moderate drag on international pricing only.
  • Neutral to mildly positive: the connected operating room (Zeiss leads on integration, clinics complain Alcon's diagnostics "do not talk to each other", Unity DX launches 2027); artificial intelligence screening, which raises detection and so procedure demand; surgical robotics (ForSight's 26 first-in-human cases), more than five years away.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D4227th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a lot of political and regulatory change is under way, and it is net mildly negative for 2027. The one large 2026 positive (the tariff refund) is non-recurring, while the negatives (reimbursement erosion, procurement pricing, residual tariffs) recur.

  • Tariffs: the Supreme Court's February 2026 ruling against tariffs imposed under emergency powers produced the $58 million Q3 refund. A temporary 10% Section 122 tariff followed, Section 301 levies of 12.5% (Switzerland) and 10% (EU) are proposed, and the company still expects import duties of 10% to 12.5%. Gross United States tariff exposure is $80 to $100 million a year on cost of goods; the 2026 net is about $40 million only because of the refund. The 2027 net headwind is therefore larger than 2026's unless relief comes, although the $40 million Q4 2026 reinvestment does not recur either (a net year-on-year swing of roughly $20 million).
  • United States reimbursement: physician fees for ophthalmic procedures fell 11% in 2026, a further 4% is proposed for 2027, and real Medicare payments have fallen over 31% in a decade. This is double-edged. It pushes surgeons toward premium conversions (a mix tailwind), but it squeezes ASC capital budgets (an equipment headwind) and discourages volume. About 41% of Alcon sales sit under fixed procedure-based reimbursement (Bernstein).
  • China: the second VBP round (H2 2026) exerts "major pricing pressure on premium implantables" (Bernstein) on 5% to 6% of group sales. Alcon won bids in seven of eight earlier categories, aims to hold China flat by volume, and plans local equipment assembly.
  • Europe: Germany prohibits private billing for premium lens procedures from 1 July 2026 (only the incremental lens cost is chargeable), a regional brake on premium adoption. EU Medical Device Regulation conformity by 2027 to 2028 adds cost.
  • Competition authorities: the Federal Trade Commission's opposition ended the LENSAR deal, which limits bolt-on M&A in surgical equipment and is a reminder that an 80%-share incumbent's bundling practices could attract scrutiny.
  • Positives: FDA approvals (Tryptyr, ENCELTO via Neurotech, BRYTLY over-the-counter drops), and wider Medicare Part D coverage for Tryptyr in 2027.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F5218th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the industry's key value driver, premium IOL penetration, is accelerating, but it is not currently accelerating Alcon's demand because Alcon is losing share of the incremental premium volume. The drivers that are accelerating Alcon's demand are in Ocular Health (prescription dry eye; preservative-free OTC drops) and, steadily rather than accelerating, the contact lens trade-up.

  • Premium IOL acceleration: United States penetration up about 200bp in H1 2026 against a 50 to 100bp historical run rate; 16% of monofocal-only surgeons plan to start offering premium lenses; Morgan Stanley sizes the 2027 uplift to the IOL market at about $300 million of value, or about 2 points of mix. Alcon's implantables nonetheless went from +1% to -2%, so the acceleration accrued mostly to PureSee, enVista and the light-adjustable lens.
  • The conversion lever, if Vivity Pro is competitive: at 66% share of a premium segment growing in the high single to low double digits, Alcon should produce implantables growth of 3% to 5% once its share stops falling. This is the swing between implantables at 0% and at +4% in 2027 to 2028 (about $70 million of revenue a year at high incremental margin).
  • Ocular Health: Tryptyr (about 5% share within twelve months; Bernstein about $110 million of 2026 sales, $250 to $400 million at peak); Systane preservative-free volumes doubled. A caution: Ocular Health missed consensus in Q3 (+8% against +10%), so the acceleration is not linear.
  • Contact lenses: daily silicone hydrogel trade-up, Precision7 (weekly; sales more than doubled year on year in Q2) and TOTAL30 Multifocal for Astigmatism sustain about +5% with about 4 points of price in the United States.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5645th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the annuity half of the thesis firmly and the premium half only conditionally. Surgeons and optometrists want more premium products, but they are currently choosing rivals' products at the margin, and patients' ability to pay is a soft constraint.

  • Surgeons: they are economically motivated to convert to premium (falling fees make standard cataract surgery "virtually unsustainable" for some clinics), and 78% intend to raise premium use. They are open to trialing competitors: PureSee's low-dysphotopsia profile is favoured for EDOF. They want proof of return before upgrading equipment ("efficiency gains hold little value" for high-volume Centurion users who already complete 30 cases by noon). Capacity remains the binding constraint on United States throughput.
  • ASCs and administrators: they demand return-on-investment justification, buy multi-vendor and push back on rigid bundles.
  • Patients: cost is the first barrier to premium adoption (Jefferies survey). Some practices report patients trading down to enhanced monofocals (80% of implants in one practice were standard or enhanced monofocals), and instalment plans are increasingly used. Premium demand is resilient among patients seeking spectacle independence, but it is income-sensitive (expert checks: penetration could fall toward 30% of a practice's cases in a recession).
  • Contact lens wearers: recurring and inelastic, trading up to dailies and silicone hydrogels.
  • Dry eye patients: growing rapidly where reimbursed; Medicare coverage for Tryptyr widens in 2027.

Fragility: low for the annuity; moderate to high for premium IOL volume and price over the next 12 months.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4326th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: net, regulators and politicians mildly hurt Alcon. The reimbursement, procurement and trade actors hurt; the drug and device approval actors help. The position is moderately fragile, because tariff and VBP outcomes can move margin by 50 to 100bp quickly.

  • Hurt: CMS (physician fees -11% in 2026, -4% proposed for 2027; a 12% cataract fee cut was proposed); China's NHSA (VBP round two in H2 2026 on premium implantables); the United States trade authorities (Section 122 at 10%, a Section 301 threat at 12.5% for Switzerland and 10% for the EU; gross exposure $80 to $100 million a year); Germany (private billing ban for premium lens procedures); the FTC (blocked LENSAR; a latent risk to bundling practices at 80% share); the EU MDR (compliance cost; conformity deadlines in 2027 to 2028).
  • Help: the FDA (approvals of Tryptyr, ENCELTO and BRYTLY; Unity 510(k) clearances); the courts (the IEEPA tariff refund); Medicare Part D coverage widening for Tryptyr in 2027; reimbursement pressure that pushes surgeons toward premium.
  • Fragility: about 41% of sales sit under fixed procedure-based reimbursement; China is 5% to 6% of sales; tariffs are equal to about 0.8% to 1% of sales gross. A severe combination (a Section 301 tariff plus a deep VBP cut plus another fee cut) could cost 100 to 150bp of margin; the base case is 20 to 40bp of net drag in 2027.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D5430th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is about $35 billion today, growing about 5% a year to about $44.6 billion by 2029. Alcon already holds about 29% of it, so its growth will track the market (4% to 6%) plus mix and adjacency, not share gains. A realistic mid-term organic growth rate is 5% to 6% a year; the company's 6% to 8% target needs share gains or acquisitions.

  • Surgical (about $13 billion: consumables $6 billion, IOLs $4 billion, equipment $3 billion): Alcon has about $5.75 billion, or about 44%. Growth of 4% to 5% (procedures 3%, plus premium mix, plus price). Premium penetration of 17% of global volumes against a possible 40% to 80% peak gives a long runway in value terms, and Morgan Stanley sizes $300 million of added IOL market value in 2027 alone.
  • Contact lenses ($11 billion): Alcon about $2.8 billion (25%). Value growth of 4% to 5% (trade-up, price, roughly 10% new wearers a year; myopia prevalence rising toward half the world's population by 2050).
  • Ocular health ($11 billion): Alcon about $1.9 billion (17%). Growth in the high single digits, led by prescription dry eye.
  • Adjacencies: retina therapeutics (Neurotech's MacTel indication is a rare disease, small), corneal cell therapy (AURN001, peak above $500 million), dry age-related macular degeneration (Valeda, $100 to $150 million peak), refractive ($1.9 billion), and myopia management (absent).
  • Mid-term arithmetic: an Alcon-weighted market of about 4.5% to 5%, plus about 0.5 to 1 point from premium mix and Ocular Health outgrowth, plus 0.5 to 1 point from bolt-ons, gives 5.5% to 7% reported. That is consistent with consensus at +5.6% a year for 2027 and 2028 and with the company tracking at the low end of 6% to 8%.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0638C-1045
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F3817th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the company's own cycle peaked in Q2 2026 and is turning down from that peak toward a mid-single-digit plateau. This is not a collapse, but the H1 2026 acceleration, powered by the Unity equipment wave and Ocular Health, is fading just as implantables reach their trough.

  • Trajectory, reported growth (FactSet): Q1 2025 +0.6%, Q2 +4.0%, Q3 +6.5%, Q4 +8.6%, Q1 2026 +9.4%, Q2 +8.7%, Q3 +4.7%. In constant currency: +3% in Q2 2025 (the trough), +6% in Q1 2026, +7% in Q2 2026 (the peak), +5% in Q3 2026, and about 5% to 6% implied for Q4.
  • Mechanics of the turn: equipment went from +23% and +25% cc in Q1 and Q2 to +11% in Q3, facing comparisons of +13% (Q3 2025) and +18% (Q4 2025), and then +23% and +25% in H1 2027. Management expects Unity unit volumes to be "stable" in 2027 with a mix shift to the lower-priced standalone Unity CS. Implantables went from +1% in Q1 and Q2 to -2% in Q3, with two more quarters of intense competitor sampling. Ocular Health slowed from +12% in Q2 to +8% in Q3 (consensus had +10%). Contact lenses are steady at +5%.
  • What turns it back up: Vivity Pro from January 2027 (management expects implantables "to return to growth in early 2027"), Medicare Part D coverage for Tryptyr in 2027, Neurotech's ENCELTO (adding 75 to 100bp to group revenue once scaled), BRYTLY (Q1 2027), and a consumables annuity that grows with the enlarged Unity base.

For the model: a 2027 shape of softer H1 growth (equipment comparisons, implantables still weak) and a firmer H2; full-year organic growth of about 4.5% to 5.5% cc, plus 0.5 to 1 point from Neurotech. That puts the company at or below the low end of its 6% to 8% long-term target, which the chief executive acknowledged on 11-Nov-26 ("tracking at the low end").

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D4532nd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Driver by driver:

  • Ageing demographics (industry and value chain): tailwind, steady, strong in volume terms (about +3% a year in procedures) and fully visible, but capped by surgeon capacity in the United States.
  • Premiumisation where the patient pays (industry): a strong and accelerating tailwind for the industry; for Alcon, contested and currently negative (share of premium falling; the sector digest rates this story "early" for Alcon).
  • Consumables as standard of care / installed-base annuity (value chain): a steady tailwind. The Unity refresh enlarges and modernises the base and supports a higher-priced consumables cycle.
  • Prescription dry eye and OTC eye care (industry): a strong tailwind, shared with Bausch + Lomb.
  • Payer pressure (United States fee cuts, China VBP, Germany's billing ban): a moderate headwind, persistent, double-edged in the United States.
  • Tariffs and reshoring (value chain): a moderate headwind, temporarily masked in 2026 by the refund.
  • Input inflation (memory chips, resins): a new mild headwind for 2027.
  • Competitive launches at the premium edge (industry): a strong headwind now, expected to persist for at least two quarters.
  • Discount rate and risk-off (sector): a strong headwind to the multiple, not to earnings.

By level: at the value-chain level, headwinds and tailwinds roughly balance (customers' incentives push premium, but their capacity and capital budgets are constrained; suppliers add mild cost pressure). At the industry level, the structure remains supportive but rivalry at the premium edge has risen materially. At the sector level, conditions are clearly negative (de-rated, relative strength F, rotation-dependent).

Overall: the external picture for the next six months is mildly negative, and worse than in the preceding six months. In H1 2026 two external headwinds of 2025 (currency, tariffs) flipped to tailwinds and flattered the beat-and-raise; those were one-offs that are now rolling off, while the one recurring external headwind that matters most (premium IOL competition) is intensifying. Volume and annuity demand remain dependable. The external environment supports a 5% to 6% grower, not a re-accelerating one.

2

Internal change forces/risks

F5625th percentile

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Alcon's internal change is a well-funded product and cost cycle run by a stable, unchanged team. It is delivering the annuity and the self-help, but it has not solved the competitive problem at the premium IOL edge, and its own ambitions have been quietly lowered. The positives are dependable and largely mechanical: the restructuring (now $130 million run-rate), the buyback (about 1% net share reduction a year), manufacturing productivity, and a broad launch slate that keeps Vision Care and Ocular Health growing at mid to high single digits. The negatives are concentrated where the multiple is set: implantables negative, guidance trimmed, 2027 margin expansion pre-guided under 100bp, and acquired pipeline assets that dilute core margins for years while their returns remain unproven (PowerVision written off; Hydrus under royalty). No leadership change, activist or strategic reset is coming to alter this. Model implication: treat internal change as supporting 10% to 12% core EPS growth from 2026 to 2028, built on revenue (about 5% to 6%), margin (+50 to +100bp a year, back-end weighted to 2028), and the share count (about -1% a year). Treat any return to the mid-20s margin as a 2029-2030 event at the earliest, and assume continued bolt-on spend that dilutes core margin by 20 to 40bp in each deal's first two years.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement57C-1457C-8638C-5952C-8162D-0366
Group judgement

Risks

B+5789th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The fundamental risk profile is moderate and well bounded. The balance sheet is strong, there are no short-seller campaigns, and the annuity is robust. The sudden fundamental risks (currency, tariffs, a Vivity Pro miss, quality or supply) each threaten 2% to 12% of EPS but are individually unlikely. The sentiment risk is high: the shares punish implantables news regardless of EPS, and the sector is de-rated and out of favour. The core engine has slowed to about its normal rate and is heading slightly below it for 2027, with the input force (procedure growth, premium share) slowing. Downside to a fair trough is about 15% to 25% and roughly balances the upside to a recovered multiple. Model implication: run a bear case with implantables at -3% in 2027 and flat in 2028, core margin flat in 2027, and a 5% dollar headwind (core EPS about 5% to 8% below base); and a tariff-escalation sensitivity of -$50 to -$150 million of EBIT.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D5735th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving clearly in cost discipline and operational manufacturing, and adequately in governance, but commercial execution at the premium IOL edge has fallen short. Improvement is real but uneven, and it is landing in the parts of the business the market is not currently testing.

  • Improving: (i) cost: total opex fell from 49% of revenue (2018) to 41% (2024); shared services grew from 200 to 2,000-2,500 staff in low-cost centres; core SG&A was 33% of sales in 2024; the restructuring target was enlarged to $130 million; artificial intelligence is being deployed in operations. (ii) Manufacturing: DSM Flex lines lowered lens unit costs; Singapore ($503 million, seven lines) and Grosswallstadt ($162 million, three lines by 2027) are under way; core gross margin rose 250bp in Q2 2026. (iii) Launch execution: Unity VCS and CS, PanOptix Pro (over 90% of United States PanOptix accounts converted), Precision7, TOTAL30 Multifocal for Astigmatism, Tryptyr, TruPlus and Unity M all launched broadly on time. (iv) Guidance discipline after 2025: conservatively set and delivered, at least through Q2.
  • Falling short: (i) commercial execution in premium IOLs: Alcon was slow to recognise RxSight as a threat (for nearly a year), is now reactive against PureSee, and its experienced equipment representatives left. (ii) Customer relationships: experts describe a "pushy", "arm-twisting" sales culture and post-sale service failures internationally. (iii) Earnings quality: restructuring costs keep rising ($150 million to $230 million), all excluded from core.
  • Governance: robust Swiss scaffolding, a new audit and risk chair with deep finance credentials. Rising whistleblower grievances (737 in 2023 to 880 in 2025, with 138 terminations) are a caution, not a red flag.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3847th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negative developments are concentrated, current and in the part of the business that sets the multiple. Several of them compound.

  • Present: (i) implantables at -2% cc and United States PC-IOL share at about 66%, with management conceding two more quarters of intense sampling; (ii) margin guidance trimmed and 2027 pre-guided at under 100bp net, against a mid-20s target; (iii) dilutive bolt-ons (Neurotech now, Aurion and LumiThera R&D continuing); (iv) cost creep in restructuring ($150 million to $230 million, all excluded from core); (v) the PowerVision write-off ($402 million) removing the long-term premium-optic option; (vi) the Sight Sciences royalty (10% of Hydrus sales until late 2028); (vii) commercial friction and the loss of experienced equipment representatives; (viii) equipment growth halving as comparisons toughen, with a lower-priced mix in 2027.
  • Plausible: (a) Vivity Pro fails to stop the share loss, implantables stay negative into H2 2027 (the "cockroach" risk: a second actual problem beside implantables, such as an impairment above $200 million, receivables growing faster than sales again, or a 2027 guide below consensus, would move the case toward park); (b) China's second VBP round cuts premium IOL prices more than expected; (c) a larger, dilutive acquisition to buy growth (a STAAR-type deal); (d) further restructuring rounds normalising "one-off" charges.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D5247th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the internal culture is stable and reasonably aligned (low turnover, good engagement, aligned pay), but the system is not fully congruent: an aggressive commercial culture that "drives productivity but triggers significant customer friction" sits uneasily with a strategy that depends on surgeon loyalty and premium pricing.

  • Congruent: pay aligned to sales, core EPS and free cash flow (87% of the chief executive's target pay is at risk); 85% of employees rate the ethical culture positively; engagement above global benchmarks; turnover falling.
  • Incongruent: "pushy", "arm-twisting" field behaviour and post-sale service gaps internationally, against a premium strategy that needs goodwill; rising whistleblower grievances (737 to 880, with 138 ethics terminations in 2025); incentives on core metrics that encourage exclusions; and an innovation culture that has not delivered the next premium optic internally.
  • Against the benchmarks named in the question (Lifco, Avanza, Ryanair): Alcon does not show a single, simple, self-reinforcing system logic. It is a well-run large corporate with some internal tensions.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B+6686th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the forecast differs little from the past in total, so few drivers of change are needed, and those that exist are diffuse and mostly mechanical. What is concentrated is not the base but the variance around it and, separately, the valuation. About three-quarters of the company-specific uncertainty in 2027 EPS comes from two drivers (premium lens share, and management's reinvestment rate), and those two partly offset each other, because management flexes the second when the first disappoints. The driver of the multiple is more concentrated still: United States premium lenses, which we estimate at only 4% to 5% of group sales. A diffuse base with concentrated, bounded and partly self-offsetting variance is a favourable profile for predicting earnings. The concentration of the valuation driver is why predictable earnings have not produced a predictable share price.

How much actually differs.

The record Our base Difference
Revenue growth a year 6.0% (2022 to 2025) 5.7% (2025 to 2028); 5.0% in 2027 Slightly slower
Core margin change a year about +70bp (2019 to 2024) +60bp (2027), +100bp (2028) The same pace
Core EPS growth a year 11.1% (2022 to 2025) 11.6% (2025 to 2028); 9.5% in 2027 The same
Share count flat at 490 to 495 million about -1% a year New
Core tax rate 17% to 18% 19% to 20% A one-time step, taken in 2026

The drivers of the differences, and how concentrated each is:

  1. The composition of revenue growth (the largest difference, and diffuse). Equipment and implantables together contributed about 1.7 points of 2026's 6.9% growth, almost all of it the Unity replacement wave. They contribute about 0.3 points in 2027. Two launches in Ocular Health (Tryptyr and ENCELTO) add about 1.1 points. The three annuity lines contribute about 3.6 points in both years, once 2026's currency gain is removed. Growth is therefore about 1 point lower after currency, and it comes from five lines, the largest contributor of which (Ocular Health, including the two launches) supplies about 40% of it.
  2. The margin (the same pace, a different composition). Gross leverage of about 150bp a year, of which half reaches the margin, as before. What differs in 2027 is a set of small, separately stated items: the non-recurrence of the tariff refund (50 to 55bp) and of a licensee milestone (10 to 15bp), Neurotech (about 20bp), chip and resin inflation (about 20bp), currency (about 10bp), and savings that are spent on inflation. No one of them exceeds 55bp.
  3. Below the operating line (new, mechanical). The buyback, a flat interest bill and a stable tax rate turn a past headwind of 1 to 3 points of EPS growth a year into a tailwind of 1.5 to 2 points.

How concentrated the EPS growth is. In 2027, revenue (five lines, three of them mechanical) supplies 5.0 of the 9.5 points of EPS growth, the margin 2.9, and the items below the line 1.2 net. In 2028 the margin's share rises to 4.7 of 12.4 points. The forecast therefore becomes more concentrated in the margin the further out it goes, and the margin is where it is least supported by the record (D-02). The same is true of consensus, more strongly: of the 43% rise in EPS from 2025 that the FactSet median for 2028 implies, about 17 points come from a 330bp rise in margin, which is one discretionary variable.

How concentrated the variance is (our estimates for FY2027; 1% of EPS is about $0.04 or $23 million of operating profit):

Driver Plausible range Effect on core EPS Nature
Core margin (reinvestment rate; input inflation) plus or minus 50bp plus or minus 2.5% Management's choice
Implantables growth (5 points of United States premium share) plus or minus 3 points plus or minus 1.7% Product cycle
Contact lens price plus or minus 1 point plus or minus 1.3% Oligopoly pricing
Equipment growth plus or minus 5 points plus or minus 0.9% Replacement cycle; lowest margin
Tryptyr and ENCELTO plus or minus $40 million and $30 million under 1% together Launches at low margin
Buyback (plus or minus $250 million) plus or minus 0.3% Mechanical
Currency (dollar plus or minus 3%) plus or minus $87 million of operating profit plus or minus 3.8% External
Tariff escalation -$50 to -$150 million -2% to -6% External; one-sided

Taken as independent, the company-specific drivers combine to about plus or minus 3.5%, and the first two account for about three-quarters of that. They are not independent in their effect on EPS, which is the important point. When revenue falls short, this management cuts or delays spending to deliver EPS (revenue below consensus in seven of the last nine quarters, EPS above in seven). When a windfall arrives, it spends most of it (two-thirds of the 2026 tariff refund). The reinvestment decision therefore acts as a stabiliser of EPS in both directions: it narrows the range of outcomes and it suppresses the upside from operating leverage. Alcon is best understood as a company that manages to an EPS number. That raises predictability and lowers the chance of a positive surprise, both at once.

How this compares with peers. The sector's 2026 disappointments came from concentrated drivers: Carl Zeiss Meditec (China procurement and capital equipment), Cooper (one segment and one channel's inventory: organic growth guide cut from 3.5%-4.5% to 2%-3%, shares down 14% in a day), Smith & Nephew (United States orthopaedics: two guidance cuts). Alcon's base has no equivalent single point of failure. Its single point of failure is in the valuation: the market reads one line as the test of the whole case.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5029th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there has been essentially no change at the top. That signals continuity and stability, but also that no new leadership force is coming to change the trajectory of the business.

  • Chief executive David Endicott (since July 2018; aged 60 at end-2025), chief financial officer Tim Stonesifer (since 2019; aged 57) and investor relations head Dan Cravens are all unchanged. The changes are a layer below or on the board: Ian Bell became chief operating officer (September 2024), Julie Brewer became President of Global Franchises (July 2025, from Baxter, Medtronic and Allergan), Kim Martin became chief human resources officer, R. Nothing signals imminent change.

For the model: no leadership-driven change in strategy or cost intensity; assume continuity of the current playbook.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5523rd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed in emphasis, not in direction. The core playbook (premiumise, protect the installed-base annuity, outspend on innovation, make bolt-on acquisitions) is unchanged, but three adjustments in 2026 are material. All are clearly articulated and well funded; one of them (buying innovation from outside rather than building it) is effectively an admission that internal premium-optic R&D has under-delivered.

  1. From building to licensing at the premium edge: after PowerVision's failure ($402 million impairment), Alcon took a non-exclusive licence from RxSight (adjustable lenses) and is relying on iterative upgrades (PanOptix Pro, Vivity Pro, TruPlus) rather than a breakthrough optic.
  2. Wider adjacency: Neurotech (October 2026, $550 million upfront; ENCELTO, the only FDA-approved cell therapy for macular telangiectasia type 2) moves Alcon into retina therapeutics. Aurion (corneal cell therapy; approval targeted for late 2028) and LumiThera (dry age-related macular degeneration) take it into cell therapy and photobiomodulation. Management says the logic is the retina surgeon channel through Constellation and Unity VCS. That is coherent by channel, but each is a new commercial capability with dilutive opex for two to three years.
  3. Operational excellence as the "second horizon": the restructuring programme was expanded in November 2026 to $230 million of costs for $130 million of run-rate savings (from $150 million and $100 million), with most of the incremental savings landing in 2027.

What has not changed but should be noted: the 2023 Capital Markets Day ambitions ($12 billion of sales and mid-20s core margins by 2027) have not been formally withdrawn, but the company now speaks of the low end of 6% to 8% growth and under 100bp of 2027 margin expansion. Consensus 2027 revenue is $11.55 billion and core EBIT margin 21.8%, so both ambitions will be missed. The strategy is resourced (R&D at the top of the 7% to 9% range; a $1.5 billion buyback; 1.4x leverage), but it is articulated more in products than in a quantified path back to the targets.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F4015th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: guidance was raised twice in H1 2026 and trimmed in November; the mid-term ambitions have been de facto lowered. On balance, guidance is moving sideways to down, and the earlier raises were driven more by non-operating items than by the business.

  • FY2026 path: the original guide (February 2026) was +5% to +7% cc sales, +70 to +170bp core margin and +9% to +12% core EPS. May raised EPS to +10% to +13% (share-count driven, from the new $1.5 billion buyback). August raised margin to +90 to +190bp and EPS to +12% to +15% (about half of the $0.07 raise at the midpoint came from $20 million of tariff refund reaching operating income). November narrowed sales to "about 6%" (the midpoint), cut margin to +100 to +150bp (midpoint 125bp against 140bp) and EPS to +12% to +14% (midpoint down 50bp), citing softer market growth and reinvestment of the refund.
  • 2027 preliminary: underlying operating leverage of 150 to 200bp, net under 100bp after Neurotech dilution and refund non-recurrence. Bernstein models +70bp. Redburn welcomed the "conservative tone".
  • Mid-term: the long-term revenue target of 6% to 8% is "maintained", but the company is "tracking at the low end". The mid-20s margin by 2027 has slipped to about 2029 or later on consensus (23.1% in 2028, 23.9% in 2029). No new Capital Markets Day is scheduled in the files.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F409th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: very little. The company is widening, not narrowing, its perimeter. The exits that have happened were forced by failure or regulation rather than chosen as simplification.

  • Exits: PowerVision (accommodating lens) discontinued for failing clinically; LENSAR terminated after FTC opposition; the STAAR Surgical bid ($1.5 billion) abandoned after shareholders opposed it; China Ocular Health commercial rights (Systane Ultra, AR-15512) out-licensed to Ocumension in October 2024 for a 16.7% stake valued at $116 million (about $40 million of annual sales).
  • Additions since 2022: Aerie (glaucoma and dry eye, $930 million), Ivantis (MIGS, $479 million), BELKIN (laser, $92 million), Cylite (diagnostics, $72 million), Aurion (corneal cell therapy, about $856 million for majority control), LumiThera (dry age-related macular degeneration, $124 million), Neurotech (retina cell therapy, $550 million), plus the RxSight licence.
  • Implication: the business is becoming more complex (pharma, cell therapy, rare disease alongside devices and consumables), each new unit runs at a loss for some years, and none of the low-return pockets (the legacy contact lens care solutions in decline, MIGS under royalty) is being exited. There is no simplification lever to model.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C5862nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: entry is active in new product markets and modest in geography. It is mostly sensible where the new market shares Alcon's existing customers or channels (dry eye through optometrists; retina through the surgical retina footprint; weekly contact lenses), and less proven where it depends on new capabilities (cell therapy, rare disease, photobiomodulation). On the core-versus-adjacency framework, most moves are "close" adjacencies by channel but "midrange" by capability.

  • Close and sensible: Precision7 weekly lenses (a $3.8 billion reusable category where Alcon was under-indexed; peak $250 to $350 million targeted); Tryptyr and Systane preservative-free (optometrist and OTC channels Alcon already serves); TruPlus enhanced monofocal (defends the standard IOL segment); BRYTLY OTC redness relief (Q1 2027, retail channels Alcon already uses); Unity CS and Unity M (larger addressable standalone-console and microscope markets).
  • Midrange, plausible but unproven: Neurotech's ENCELTO (adds 75 to 100bp to group revenue once scaled; slightly dilutive to 2027 core earnings); Aurion's AURN001 (Phase 3; approval targeted late 2028; peak above $500 million per Redburn); LumiThera Valeda ($100 to $150 million peak expected).
  • A gap left open: child myopia management, where CooperVision's MiSight leads and Alcon is absent; the STAAR deal would have covered it.
  • Geography: China localisation (equipment assembly, neutral-site lens production for tariffs), a mature European footprint, Asia-Pacific through the PanOptix Pro roll-out (Europe, Japan and Korea in H2 2026).
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C+5468th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible?

Judgement: real price increases are being achieved in United States contact lenses and in parts of Ocular Health; prices are broadly holding in consumables and equipment; and real price is negative in premium IOLs and in China. Group-level real price is modestly positive (roughly 1% to 2% of sales a year), concentrated in Vision Care and the United States.

  • Real and evidenced: contact lenses grew 5% in Q2 2026, of which about 4 points came from price; the United States grew 8% on two price increases; Q3 contact lenses grew 5% "driven by favorable pricing and premium mix in the United States". Annual list price increases of about 2% are standard practice. In 2025 Alcon offset an $80 million tariff headwind "through proactive pricing adjustments and supply efficiencies". Systane's premium preservative-free formats carry higher prices per unit.
  • Holding: consumables (bundled packs; some net-price pressure as clinics unbundle); equipment (Unity commands a premium, but the 2027 mix shifts to the lower-priced Unity CS).
  • Negative: premium IOLs (PureSee about 15% below Vivity; Bausch + Lomb lenses about $100 cheaper; free trials pressure average selling prices); China (VBP); international contact lenses (online price transparency, private label; Q2 international growth only +3%).
  • Latent: the higher-priced consumables cycle tied to Unity (Jefferies) has not yet been demonstrated in reported consumables growth (+5%).

For the model: price of +1.5% to +2% a year in Vision Care, about 0% to +1% in surgical consumables, -1% to -3% a year on premium IOL average selling price until Vivity Pro is proven.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4210th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A is steady rather than stepped up: frequent bolt-ons of $50 to $600 million aimed at pipeline and adjacency, after two larger surgical deals (STAAR, LENSAR) failed. Strategic sense is medium, price discipline is decent (management walked away from STAAR and LENSAR), but the record on returns is weak, and the cumulative effect is to keep core margins below target through acquired operating losses.

  • Spend: about $3 billion since 2022 (Aerie $930 million, Ivantis $479 million in 2022, BELKIN $92 million, Cylite $72 million, Aurion about $856 million, LumiThera $124 million, Neurotech $550 million, RxSight $60 million upfront); acquisitions of $648 million in FY2025 alone (FactSet). Deutsche Bank says Alcon "continues to actively evaluate" tuck-ins of $50 to $500 million.
  • Returns so far: PowerVision (bought 2019) written off ($402 million net, $505 million gross); Ivantis' Hydrus hit by the Sight Sciences judgment ($55.4 million in damages plus a 10% royalty until patent expiry in late 2028) and soft MIGS sales; Aerie's Tryptyr growing (about $110 million in 2026) but years from paying back $930 million; Aurion (Bernstein: about 80bp of core margin pressure from its R&D) not commercial until 2028 or later.
  • Discipline: walking away from STAAR (a $1.5 billion deal opposed by shareholders, criticised as "conflict-ridden" because STAAR's chair was a former Alcon consultant) and LENSAR (FTC) preserved cash, which went to buybacks.
  • Integration: no visible integration failures in the files, but the acquired assets are mostly pre-revenue, so integration has not really been tested.

For the model: assume $300 to $600 million a year of bolt-on spend that dilutes core margin by 20 to 40bp in its first two years (the Aurion precedent was about 40bp; Neurotech is described as "slightly dilutive").

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

B+7087th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns have clearly stepped up, from a spin-off company that barely bought back stock to a $1.5 billion three-year programme executed at historically low multiples, with balance-sheet room for more. This is the most dependable self-help EPS lever in the case.

  • History: cash dividends paid rose from nil (2019 and 2020) to $166 million (2025). A $750 million buyback was completed in January 2026 (9,301,877 shares). The new $1.5 billion three-year programme started in May 2026. Returns: $848 million in 2025; $748 million year to date in 2026 (including $174 million of dividends; about $574 million of buybacks, of which $210 million was in Q3); about $1.0 billion of the authorisation remains.
  • Capacity: net debt of about $3.25 billion on the company's measure ($3.53 billion on FactSet's, including leases) at 1.3x to 1.4x EBITDA, projected toward about 0.9x by 2027 on earnings growth. Debt is 98% fixed-rate with an 8.8-year average maturity, the $1.32 billion revolving facility is undrawn, and free cash flow runs at $1.6 to $1.8 billion a year against dividends of about $175 million.
  • Net effect on share count: the buyback runs at about 1.4% of market capitalisation a year (about $500 million on $37 billion), but equity grants exceed vesting (by 400,000 shares in Q1 2026), so the net reduction is about 1% a year. Guidance uses 492 million average shares for 2026; consensus implies about 483 million in 2027 and 479 million in 2028.
  • What has not happened: no leveraged acceleration, special dividend or material dividend growth (yield about 0.5%). Management keeps "organic investment first" and reserves capacity for bolt-ons.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–57

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several positive forces are present; their combined effect is meaningful for 2027 to 2029 earnings but small for the 2027 implantables test.

  • The innovation staircase: Vivity Pro (January 2027); Unity DX diagnostics and the full connected Unity ecosystem (2027); Unity M microscope (late 2026); BRYTLY (Q1 2027); PanOptix Pro international (H2 2026 onward); Precision7 and TOTAL30 Multifocal for Astigmatism ramps. More than 80 pipeline products and 10 to 15 launches over two years.
  • Teaching-hospital seeding: Unity is placed in 40% of United States teaching hospitals within a year, with brand exposure across 98% of ophthalmic teaching programmes, which builds lifetime surgeon loyalty (the Centurion precedent: 21 of 47 surveyed phaco machines are still Centurions a decade on).
  • Cost and capital: $130 million run-rate savings (most of the increment in 2027); amortisation of spin-off intangibles running down (helps IFRS earnings, not core); manufacturing footprint moves to cut tariff exposure.
  • Optionality: the RxSight adjustable presbyopia-correcting lens; AURN001 (peak above $500 million); a Systane franchise that management believes can reach $1 billion; the surgeon-capacity release (external).
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C-5051st percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: very little. Alcon has no controlling shareholder and no activist, and the one prominent holder (Pershing Square) has signalled support for the current strategy rather than pressure for change.

  • Pershing Square added Alcon during the 2026 de-rating, citing mid-single-digit market growth, the installed-base annuity, and operating margins able to expand toward 25% or more; it notes that 8% historical earnings growth since the spin "understates" the potential. That is an endorsement of margin delivery, and implicitly pressure for it, not a demand for strategic change.
  • Swiss governance (one share, one vote; no opt-out of the mandatory takeover bid above 33.3%; binding annual pay votes) and a dispersed register (Swiss registered holders 21.46%) mean change would come only through the board.
  • STAAR's shareholders (Broadwood Partners) blocked Alcon's bid, which shows that outside shareholders can constrain Alcon's M&A but not its strategy.
  • Implication: no actor is positioned to force portfolio simplification, a faster margin push or larger capital returns. The upside scenario of an activist-led cost push (as Cevian at Smith & Nephew) is absent.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6141st percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Taken together, how strong, broad and well resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: broad and well resourced, but only moderately strong in their effect on the line that matters. The positive changes span five product lines, a cost programme, a buyback and a twice-raised (then trimmed) guide. They are well funded (R&D about 9% of sales, capex about 5% to 6%, $1.5 billion of buybacks, 1.4x leverage), and most are delivering: equipment, Ocular Health, contact lenses, gross margin, shareholder returns. Their weakness is that they do not yet reach implantables, where the market has placed its test, and that leadership, strategy and structure are unchanged, so there is no new force to raise the trajectory beyond about 5% to 6% organic growth and 50 to 100bp of annual margin expansion. They are strong enough to sustain core EPS growth of about 10% to 12% a year through 2028 (about 5.5% revenue, 3% to 4% margin, 1% buyback) without heroics. They are not strong enough, on present evidence, to restore the 2027 Capital Markets Day ambitions or to force a re-rating before implantables turn.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C5065th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: there is a real, dated staircase, but its first step (the January 2027 Vivity Pro launch and the claimed early-2027 return to implantables growth) is the one that matters most and the least certain. Most of the other steps are incremental or already in consensus. The longer-dated steps (AURN001, the end of the Hydrus royalty, mid-20s margins, the adjustable lens) are novel but distant.

  • Next 6 months (to May 2027):
  • Vivity Pro, United States launch, January 2027: highly novel (Alcon's first new EDOF since Vivity); management-dated; founding evidence is moderate, with no head-to-head data yet. Surgeon feedback from expert calls and surveys should come in February to April. This is the pivotal step.
  • Q4 2026 results and the FY2027 guide (2 March 2027 per FactSet): low novelty (pre-signalled under 100bp), and probably a second negative implantables quarter, so a net negative headline risk unless the guide surprises.
  • BRYTLY OTC (Q1 2027), Unity DX and the Unity M roll-out: modest novelty, small financial effect.
  • Medicare Part D coverage for Tryptyr widening in 2027: moderately novel, well founded, and it unlocks direct-to-consumer marketing.
  • Q1 2027 results (May 2027): the first test of "return to growth early 2027".
  • Months 6 to 18 (to May 2028):
  • Two or more quarters of positive implantables growth (H2 2027 laps a -2% Q3 2026): novel for the market and the trigger for a re-rating on sector precedent; moderately well founded (easy comparisons plus Vivity Pro).
  • Equipment comparisons ease in H2 2027; Unity CS penetrates ASCs.
  • Restructuring savings of $130 million fully in run rate.
  • Neurotech's ENCELTO scaling; PanOptix Pro international; Grosswallstadt lines complete (2027).
  • A possible reset of mid-term targets (Capital Markets Day; undated): novel either way.
  • Months 18 to 36 (to November 2029):
  • AURN001 approval (targeted late 2028) and launch (peak above $500 million per Redburn): novel, moderately well founded (Phase 3 started April 2026; approved in Japan as Vyznova).
  • The Hydrus patent expires in late 2028, so the 10% royalty to Sight Sciences ends: small, certain.
  • The RxSight-Alcon adjustable presbyopia-correcting lens: novel, undated, early.
  • Margins approaching 23% to 24%; amortisation run-off lifting IFRS EPS; Systane toward $1 billion.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B+7487th percentile

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B+7487th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Alcon operates in one of the most supportive industry structures in medtech: concentrated oligopolies with high barriers, recurring consumables, an ageing customer base and patient-paid premium tiers. It leads almost every category it competes in. The support is uneven, though: robust in contact lenses, consumables and equipment (about three-quarters of revenue), and fragile at the premium IOL edge, where share rotates on product cycles and Johnson & Johnson is now running a sustained, volume-first attack timed to Alcon's weakest moment. Competitor behaviour is benign in contact lenses (where Johnson & Johnson and Cooper are weakening) and aggressive in premium IOLs (Johnson & Johnson, Bausch + Lomb). Regulators and payers apply moderate, recurring pressure, and customers are consolidating and gaining bargaining power slowly. On sector precedent, Alcon's shares re-rate only when the named worry visibly clears, and it has not. Model implication: model contact lenses and consumables with tight ranges (+5% to +6% and +4% to +6% a year respectively, with the share trend slightly favourable). Model premium IOLs with a wide range: base case implantables of 0% to +2% in 2027 (Vivity Pro offsetting annualising share loss), +3% in 2028, with a bear range of -3% and a bull range of +5%. Assume no category price war, but continued average-selling-price erosion of 1% to 3% in premium IOLs until a share stabilisation is proven.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
