C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5645th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C6461st percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the direct route to surgeons is a strength (4,100 sales associates, Alcon-employed technicians, about 45 training centres, the teaching-hospital presence). The intermediated routes in contact lenses and Ocular Health are modest hindrances that are slowly growing. Overall the route to market is slightly helpful today and slowly getting less so.

  • Helping: direct surgical selling and service; bundling of consoles, packs and lenses; training and teaching-hospital placements (Unity in 40% of United States teaching hospitals); independent optometrists as a high-margin channel for premium contact lenses; the BlinkRx specialty pharmacy platform for Tryptyr.
  • Hindering: (i) large optical retailers, club stores (Costco) and buying groups demand volume discounts and resist list-price rises; (ii) online channels and private label create international price transparency; (iii) optometrists' business models rely on optical retail, so prescribing drops that do not generate in-practice revenue meets resistance (relevant to Tryptyr); (iv) private-equity-owned ASCs centralise purchasing and unbundle; (v) Alcon's own field force is described as aggressive and inconsistent by geography, with post-sale service gaps internationally.
  • Fragility: moderate. The channel shift is gradual, but bundling power is the mechanism that protected surgical share, and it is eroding at the margin.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C-5056th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors are hurting Alcon in premium IOLs and helping it in contact lenses. Alcon's position is robust against all of them in consoles and consumables, and fragile against Johnson & Johnson in United States premium IOLs for the next two to four quarters.

  • Johnson & Johnson Vision: aggressive in IOLs (Odyssey, PureSee; United States surgical +14% in Q3), but losing contact lens share (to about 37.6% by one measure). Its overall vision growth is healthy (+5.6% in Q2, +5.1% in Q3).
  • Bausch + Lomb: strong pharmaceutical growth (Miebo $91 million in Q2, +44%), which competes with Tryptyr for the same dry eye prescriptions; recovering enVista premium IOLs; daily silicone hydrogels +25%. Constrained by a 5% surgical margin and high leverage, so it cannot outspend Alcon's R&D.
  • CooperVision: weak (flat organic Q3 on United States destocking; organic guide cut to 2% to 3%), which helps Alcon's contact lens share. Cooper has no IOL business.
  • Carl Zeiss Meditec: weakened (China VBP, Americas -12.7%, ProfitUp restructuring, new chief executive, de-rated to 16x), and is fighting through digital workflow integration and EVA NEXUS. A smaller threat than a year ago.
  • RxSight: innovative but commercially stumbling; now partly a partner.
  • Glaukos and Sight Sciences (MIGS): competitive contracting; a 10% royalty on Hydrus to Sight Sciences.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D4326th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4849th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the disruption forces act mostly on Alcon's customers (surgeons' economics and capacity) and on its competitive set (optic technology), and only lightly on its suppliers and channels. Together they leave the annuity robust and the premium edge fragile.

  • Demand side (surgeons and ASCs): falling fees and staff shortages squeeze practice economics and throughput, which (i) pushes premium conversion (helps), (ii) caps volume (hurts consumables growth), (iii) delays capital purchases (hurts equipment), and (iv) shifts purchasing to administrators (erodes bundling). Artificial intelligence screening and digital diagnostics can raise throughput and detection, a mild positive.
  • Competitors: the non-diffractive EDOF and light-adjustable lenses shift the technology frontier away from Alcon's diffractive strength, the largest single fragility.
  • Regulators and politicians: tariffs act on cost and footprint (Alcon is moving equipment assembly into China and lens production to neutral sites); VBP acts on price in China.
  • Suppliers: memory-chip and resin inflation (from the artificial intelligence hardware boom and petrochemicals) adds cost; single-source dependencies remain.
  • Channels: online contact lens ordering and private label erode international pricing; Alcon's MARLO app and digital investment are a defensive answer.
  • Fragility: overall moderate. The consumables and contact lens annuity is little exposed; the premium IOL position is highly exposed for 12 to 18 months.

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement74C-2480C-2590C-2664C-2767C-2862C-2940C-3174C-3566C-3676
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)B+7487th percentilefull answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B8079th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Alcon is number one in global eye care overall and in most of its categories, and number two in contact lenses. Its leadership is widest where the revenue is recurring (installed base and consumables) and narrowest, and currently shrinking, in premium IOLs.

  • Global eye care: number one, with $10.3 billion of sales, more than three times the nearest pure-play surgical competitor. Against a $35 billion market that is about 29% share.
  • IOLs ($4 billion market): number one at 45%, ahead of Johnson & Johnson (27%), Carl Zeiss Meditec (7%) and Bausch + Lomb (5%). In United States presbyopia-correcting IOLs: about 66% (late 2026), down from over 80% (2021 to 2024). In global premium volume (Jefferies survey): 66% in 2026, with Johnson & Johnson at 18% and RxSight at 10%.
  • Phaco and vitreoretinal equipment: number one, with more than 80% of the United States installed base and about 50% globally (30,000 units).
  • Surgical consumables ($6 billion): number one, about $3.0 billion of sales (half the market), tied to the installed base.
  • Contact lenses ($11 billion): number two, at 24% to 26% (a record of about 26% in late 2025), behind Johnson & Johnson (32% to 37.6% depending on the source and declining) and level with CooperVision (24%); Bausch + Lomb 9%. The gap to number one is about 8 to 12 points and narrowing slowly.
  • Ocular Health / OTC eye care ($11 billion): a leading OTC position (Systane); a newcomer in prescription dry eye (Tryptyr, about 5% share), behind Bausch + Lomb's Xiidra and Miebo.
  • Refractive ($1.9 billion): joint number two with STAAR and Johnson & Johnson at 17%, behind Zeiss (38%); under 3% of Alcon's revenue.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A9097th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: extremely concentrated, and very steep. In IOLs the top four hold about 85% (the top five over 90%), and the leader alone holds 45%. In contact lenses the top four hold about 90%, with private label and regional players sharing the remaining 10%. In surgical equipment Alcon alone holds over 80% of the United States base. The top five hold many times the share of numbers six to ten in every category. Refractive is a specialised three to four player market. The only fragmented pockets are Chinese domestic IOL makers (relevant only within VBP) and MIGS, where Glaukos, Sight Sciences and Alcon compete. This structure supports rational pricing in contact lenses and in standard surgical consumables, high regulatory and clinical barriers to entry, and stable profit pools. Its one fragility is that in a technology-led premium segment, concentration does not stop share from moving quickly between the incumbents when one launches a better optic.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C-6456th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: stable in contact lenses, consumables and equipment; highly unstable in premium IOLs, where shares swing on product cycles. New entrants have taken meaningful share only at the premium IOL edge (the light-adjustable lens) and at the low end of contact lenses (private label).

  • Contact lenses: slow, directional drift. Johnson & Johnson loses about a point a year; Alcon and Cooper gain. Alcon gained 700bp in torics (to a 22% specialty share) over 2020 to 2024. Private label and regional makers hold about 10%. Capacity constraints in daily silicone hydrogels historically cost some makers private-label contracts.
  • Equipment and consumables: very stable, because installed bases turn over on seven to ten-year cycles. Centurion still dominates a decade after launch.
  • Premium IOLs: Alcon's United States PC-IOL share went from 55% (2009) to 35% (2019), to over 80% (2021, PanOptix), to 66% (Q1 2025, Odyssey and Envy), to 70-74% (PanOptix Pro), and back to about 66% (late 2026, PureSee). That amplitude, 30 to 45 points within a few years, is extreme for a device segment.
  • New entrants: RxSight's light-adjustable lens went from nothing before 2019 to about 10% of premium volume (survey), rising to 12% expected in 2027. In China, domestic makers take public-hospital volume through VBP. No other new entrant has scaled.

For the model: a share forecast for premium IOLs carries a wide error band (plus or minus 5 points in either direction over 12 to 18 months), while contact lens and consumables shares can be held near current levels with a slight upward drift.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C+6769th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in the annuity categories share consolidates toward the largest and most advantaged makers, and Alcon is the principal beneficiary. In premium IOLs share goes to whoever has the best current optic, and Alcon is not presently that maker.

  • Contact lenses: share consolidates toward makers with silicone hydrogel daily capacity, R&D and eye-care-professional relationships: Alcon (water gradient, Precision1, Total30, Precision7) and Cooper, at Johnson & Johnson's expense. Capacity is the gate: Alcon's Singapore and Grosswallstadt lines and DSM Flex give it the most scalable, lowest-cost capacity.
  • Equipment and consumables: share consolidates toward the installed-base owner through bundling, workflow standardisation and training (45 centres; 98% of United States teaching programmes exposed to Unity). Alcon is the dominant beneficiary; Zeiss is losing ground (Americas -12.7%), and the FTC's LENSAR decision shows that regulators may stop Alcon buying the remaining independents.
  • Premium IOLs: advantage is technology-led and temporary (12 to 24 months of lead per generation, as in hearing aids). Scale helps through R&D spend and bundling, but it did not stop Johnson & Johnson's Odyssey and PureSee or Bausch + Lomb's enVista from taking share. Alcon is among the largest but not currently the most advantaged.
  • Net: about three-quarters of Alcon's revenue sits in categories where consolidation works in its favour. The quarter that does not is the most profitable.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-6258th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in contact lenses and standard surgical consumables competitors behave rationally and put margin first. In premium IOLs, Johnson & Johnson and Bausch + Lomb are currently putting volume first, and Chinese domestic makers do so in VBP. Industry prices have not fallen when volume softened. Price pressure is targeted and product-led, not a category price war.

  • Contact lenses: experts call the industry "highly civilized, with high barriers, preventing aggressive low-cost price wars". Annual price increases of 1% to 3% stick in the United States. Cooper expects 1% to 3% pricing a year. Even with flat volumes at Cooper in Q3 (destocking), no one cut price.
  • Premium IOLs: Johnson & Johnson prices PureSee about 15% below Vivity in Europe and offers free trials and discounted clinical sampling; Bausch + Lomb lenses are about $100 cheaper and it uses "aggressive financial bundling" (and copies Alcon's bundling and medical education playbook). Bausch + Lomb's surgical operating margin was 5% in H1 2026, so it is buying share at the expense of its own margin. This is volume-first behaviour, but with healthy list prices across the segment, so premium remains a high-value category.
  • China: VBP drives price cuts of 50% to 60% in recent rounds (80% to 90% historically); domestic makers accept them for volume.
  • Do prices fall with volume? No. United States cataract volumes were flat in 2026 while contact lens and consumable prices held. The price pressure in premium IOLs is a share fight, not a response to falling demand.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4030th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years?

Judgement: the attitude has changed in premium IOLs over the last two years, from a long period in which Alcon's lead went largely uncontested to a sustained, deliberate share attack by Johnson & Johnson (Odyssey in late 2024, then PureSee in Europe in 2024 and the United States in March 2026). The attack has the hallmarks of a "shark attack" that intensifies when the incumbent looks weak: free trials, discounts, and targeted pursuit of private-practice accounts during Alcon's implantables trough. Johnson & Johnson's behaviour is strategic and consistent rather than "O'Leary"-unstable: it is a well-funded corporate putting priority on its vision franchise. Bausch + Lomb's behaviour is more opportunistic (aggressive after its enVista recall in March 2025). Contact lens attitudes have been stable for years and show no sign of change, even as Cooper weakens (guide cut to 2% to 3% organic; destocking), though a weakened Cooper becoming more promotional is a tail risk worth watching. Risk: if Vivity Pro underwhelms in H1 2027, expect Johnson & Johnson to press harder on price and sampling in the United States EDOF segment, extending implantables weakness into H2 2027 or beyond.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B+7487th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is highly supportive overall (concentrated oligopolies, high clinical and regulatory barriers, recurring consumables, patient-paid premium tiers, ageing demand). The support is fragile in exactly one place: the premium IOL edge, which is technology-led, rotates between incumbents and is under attack now.

  • Robust: contact lenses (rational oligopoly, pricing power in the United States, trade-up mix); surgical consumables (installed-base lock-in; consumables grow at roughly procedure growth plus price); equipment (80% United States base, ten-year cycles); OTC eye care (brands, retail distribution).
  • Fragile: premium IOLs (about half of implantables value and a much larger share of the segment's profit). Here concentration does not protect share because surgeons trial and switch lenses case by case; switching costs are low relative to consoles; and price is visible to patients paying out of pocket.
  • Slower-moving risks to the support: customer consolidation (private-equity ASCs, buying groups) eroding bundling power; payers (Medicare cuts, VBP, Germany); FTC scrutiny of bundling at 80% share.

For the model: consumables and contact lenses can be modelled with low error; implantables need a wide range (see C-26).

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?B6682nd percentilefull answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A7694th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a low threat and not rising materially. Cataract has no non-surgical substitute: an opacified lens must be replaced. For presbyopia, the alternatives (reading glasses, presbyopia drops such as Vuity, LENZ and Yuvezzi, monovision) compete with premium IOLs only at the margin; the drops are struggling commercially and with provider adoption. For myopia, the alternatives to contact lenses are spectacles, refractive surgery (LASIK and SMILE, where Zeiss leads; implantable collamer lenses from STAAR, which Alcon tried and failed to buy) and myopia management for children (CooperVision's MiSight; Alcon is absent). Refractive volumes have been weak since the post-pandemic peak, so this substitution is not accelerating. In dry eye, prescription alternatives (Miebo, Xiidra, Restasis-type drugs) are competitors rather than substitutes. One alternative is rising: enhanced monofocals (TruPlus; Johnson & Johnson's Eyhance-type designs) as a cheaper substitute for presbyopia-correcting lenses. Alcon participates through TruPlus, but this substitution lowers average selling price per premium-intent patient.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

B+7088th percentile8 answers
Judgement70C-4172C-4280C-4354C-4465C-4561C-4625C-4772
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

B+7088th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Alcon's competitive advantage is broad, deep and largely self-reinforcing in the annuity parts of eye care (consoles and consumables, contact lenses, OTC drops), where scale, an installed base, manufacturing cost and training ecosystems compound mechanically and are widening. It is narrower and currently weakening at the premium IOL edge, where advantage is technology-led, scale does not guarantee the winning optic, and Alcon's premium pricing without scale-sharing invites price-led attacks. Pricing power is real in United States contact lenses and was used to absorb tariffs, but it is absent in premium IOLs and China. The addressable market (about $35 billion, growing about 5%) and Alcon's 29% share mean growth will track the market plus mix and bolt-ons. Model implication: a structurally safe floor of about 4% to 5% revenue growth and about 8% to 10% EPS growth from the mechanical engine. The 2 to 4 points of extra EPS growth that consensus assumes for 2028 depend on regaining premium IOL momentum and on margin, which are the least moat-protected parts of the business.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

B+7287th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Alcon holds strong, widening moats in its annuity businesses (installed base, consumables, contact lens manufacturing, training ecosystem) and a weakening technology moat at the premium IOL edge. The aggregate moat is strong, but it protects the low-growth, steady parts of the business better than the high-margin part the market is watching.

  • Customer-related, installed-base and workflow lock-in (strong, widening near term): more than 80% of the United States phaco base; consoles lock clinics into proprietary single-use cassettes and packs for seven to ten years; switching means retraining whole surgical teams and introduces intraoperative risk; the Vision Suite connects diagnostics to consoles. The Unity refresh renews the base for another decade, and placements in 40% of United States teaching hospitals shape the next generation's preferences. Narrowing slightly at the edges as private-equity ASCs unbundle.
  • Training and clinical relationships (strong, widening): about 45 training centres; a direct force of 4,100; proctoring of 15 to 20 retinal cases per surgeon. Offset by complaints about pushy commercial behaviour.
  • Production and scale, contact lenses (strong, widening): about 90% in-house manufacturing; DSM Flex modular lines (lower unit cost, faster prototyping, lower capex); the $503 million Singapore and $162 million Grosswallstadt lines. This let Alcon reach a record ~26% share while Cooper struggles.
  • Scale in R&D (strong in amount, narrowing in effect): $990 million in FY2025, more than double the combined budgets of the closest pure-play peers, and 2,650 patent families. But it did not produce the winning optic in EDOF (PureSee), accommodating lenses (PowerVision failed) or adjustability (licensed from RxSight). On the "innovation only counts if cumulative" test, the cumulative part is in materials and manufacturing (water gradient, Clareon), not in premium optics.
  • Regulatory (medium, stable): Class III approvals, clinical evidence and the EU MDR raise barriers to new entrants, but not to Johnson & Johnson or Bausch + Lomb.
  • Brands (medium, stable): Systane, Dailies, PanOptix and Precision1 command shelf and prescriber loyalty.
  • Premium IOL technology (medium, narrowing): United States PC-IOL share is down from over 80% to about 66%. PanOptix Pro's 94% light utilisation and good reading vision are offset by glare and halo complaints, and Vivity is losing EDOF cases. Whether this moat re-widens depends on Vivity Pro (January 2027).
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A8094th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Alcon's scale is the largest in eye care by a wide margin. It confers cost and reach advantages that are real and compounding in consumables, contact lens manufacturing and commercial coverage, but it does not confer technology leadership at the premium edge.

  • Size: $10.3 billion of 2025 revenue, more than three times the nearest pure-play surgical rival; about 25,000 employees (2,000 shared-services staff are 8% of headcount); 16 to 17 plants; 4,100 sales associates; presence in 56 countries and sales in over 140; R&D of $990 million; free cash flow of $1.6 to $1.7 billion.
  • What it confers: (i) the ability to bundle console, consumables and lenses (rivals such as LENSAR, which has no IOL portfolio, cannot); (ii) unit-cost advantages in contact lenses; (iii) shared-services SG&A leverage (opex fell from 49% of revenue in 2018 to 41% in 2024); (iv) outspending rivals on launches and training (Bausch + Lomb's 5% surgical margin cannot match an $800 million-plus R&D budget); (v) balance-sheet capacity for bolt-ons and buybacks; (vi) clinical-evidence generation.
  • What it does not confer: protection from a better optic. Johnson & Johnson's vision franchise is smaller in surgery but backed by a parent with far greater resources.
  • Bounded-market test: the scale advantage is real where the market is bounded and the minimum efficient scale is high (consoles and installed base, silicone hydrogel lens production), and weak where innovation resets the race (premium optics).
C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?D5430th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C+6572nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: inferiority can be disproved in equipment, consumables, contact lenses and OTC eye care, where Alcon leads. It cannot currently be disproved in EDOF premium IOLs, where surgeons' revealed preference has moved to PureSee. Overall utility is high but no longer uncontested at the premium edge.

  • Equipment (leading): Unity's 4D Phaco removes the nucleus up to twice as fast with 41% less energy; Intelligent Sentry stabilises the anterior chamber; workflow lets a surgeon do 21 cases a day instead of 20; Deutsche Bank cites up to $250,000 of annual clinic savings; the Unity M microscope offers a red-reflex zone up to six times larger than Zeiss ARTEVO. Caveat: high-volume Centurion users see little marginal value, and buyers doubt the savings claims.
  • Trifocal IOLs (not inferior, with caveats): PanOptix Pro delivers 94% light utilisation and 50% less scatter than PanOptix; global PanOptix family sales grew 9% in Q3 2026; but glare and halo complaints persist.
  • EDOF IOLs (currently inferior on revealed preference): surgeons prefer PureSee's "aberration-free and dysphotopsia-free" intermediate vision and are moving Vivity cases; Vivity Pro (January 2027) is the answer, with no head-to-head data published.
  • Contact lenses (leading): water gradient technology (Dailies Total1, Total30, the only monthly multifocal toric) and Precision7 (the weekly schedule preferred by optometrists for compliance).
  • Dry eye (competitive): Tryptyr works from Day 1; Systane's preservative-free formats lead OTC. Competition from Miebo and Xiidra is strong.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B6179th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: pricing power is demonstrated in United States contact lenses, OTC eye care and, in 2025, in passing through tariffs. It is absent or negative in premium IOLs and China. Alcon uses its power where it has it (annual increases of about 2%; two United States contact lens increases in 2026), without visible loss of contact lens share, which reached a record. In IOLs, holding price against a 15% cheaper rival is costing share.

  • Applied and held: contact lenses +5% in Q2 2026, of which about 4 points was price (United States +8%), and Q3 growth was again "driven by favorable pricing"; Cooper by contrast expects only 1% to 3%. Alcon offset an $80 million tariff headwind in 2025 through pricing and efficiencies. Systane premium formats. Unity consoles at premium prices with financing.
  • Given back: premium IOLs, where competitors undercut and offer free trials and Alcon has not cut list price but is losing share (the economic equivalent of price erosion). China VBP cuts of 50% to 60%. International contact lenses ("pricing was harder to achieve abroad").
  • Balance: the business mix with about 60% of revenue paid out of pocket by patients is structurally better for pricing than reimbursed medtech (Bernstein's private-against-public test), but Alcon's premium IOL price premium is now being tested.

For the model: price contribution of about +1% to +1.5% of group sales a year (+2% to +4% in Vision Care United States; flat to slightly negative in surgical premium; negative in China).

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F2511th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: little. Alcon is a premium-price, value-capture company, not a scale-economies-sharing one. The lack of sharing is starting to cost it, because rivals are entering through price (PureSee about 15% below Vivity; Bausch + Lomb lenses about $100 cheaper) and customers resent the bundling.

  • Evidence against sharing: expert panels report "monopolistic" bundling, "arm-twisting" and accusations of "gouging" providers; surgeons view Alcon's premium lenses as poor value for their high price; retail chains resist its price increases; management raised contact lens prices twice in 2026 and holds IOL list prices.
  • Partial, de facto sharing: Unity's efficiency gains (time, energy, staff) and the claimed $250,000 of annual savings pass value to clinics; tiered contact lens brands across price points; patient payment programmes and manufacturer-sponsored trials; the Unity DX six-in-one analyser lowers clinics' capital cost.
  • Implication: Alcon's model is to capture surplus from its scale and installed base rather than reinvest it in lower prices. That maximises near-term margin but leaves the flank open to share attacks at the premium edge, where switching costs are lowest. The Wisdom Library's scale-economies-shared test would grade this business as a capturer, not a sharer.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B+7287th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: to a substantial extent for about 70% of revenue and for the share count. The installed base, ageing demand, the contact lens trade-up, manufacturing cost curves and buybacks compound with little heroics. The remaining 30% (implantables, equipment, new pharma launches) depends on product wins that require dynamism, and that is the part currently disappointing.

  • Mechanical: (i) consumables (about 29% of sales) grow with procedures and the installed base, at about +5% a year, with the Unity base lifting average selling prices; (ii) contact lenses (about 27%) grow 5% to 6% from trade-up and price; (iii) OTC Ocular Health grows with brand and an ageing population; (iv) lower contact lens unit costs as new lines fill; (v) shared-services leverage; (vi) buybacks cut the share count about 1% a year; (vii) net debt falls on cash generation; (viii) spin-off amortisation runs off (helps IFRS earnings; not core).
  • Requires dynamism: implantables share (product cycles: Vivity Pro, adjustable lenses); equipment (the replacement wave fades after 2026); Tryptyr, Neurotech and Aurion launches; holding pricing against rivals.
  • Against peers: Alcon's mechanical engine is larger and steadier than Cooper's (contact lenses only, now destocking), Zeiss's (capital-equipment and China heavy) or Bausch + Lomb's (leveraged), comparable to Sonova's (platform-cycle dependent), and better than Smith & Nephew's (orthopaedic share donor).
  • Arithmetic: the mechanical engine alone delivers group revenue growth of about 4% to 5% and core EPS growth of about 8% to 10% a year (revenue, modest operating leverage from shared services and cost savings, and a 1% buyback), even with implantables and equipment flat.

Group judgement

Company - Management

D5750th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a capable, stable, well-resourced incumbent team that builds and runs the business well, communicates clearly in the near term and governs prudently. It falls short of outlier status in two areas that matter for this case: anticipating competitive shifts at the premium IOL edge, and earning returns on acquired capital. The chief executive's record since the spin is good on margin, launches and the spin itself, and mixed on 2025 guidance, PowerVision and M&A the team has orderly succession but some status-quo risk. Model implication: trust management's near-term building blocks (they are specific and, since 2026, conservatively set), but haircut mid-term ambitions (assume the low end of 6% to 8% revenue growth and mid-20s margins only by about 2030). C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

C6663rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the ambition is large and growth-oriented (global leadership in every eye care category; outspending all rivals on R&D expanding into adjacent therapeutics), and it is greater than most peers'. But ambition has outrun delivery: the 2027 targets ($12 billion of sales; mid-20s core margin) will be missed, and the language has shifted to "the low end of 6% to 8%".

  • Evidence of ambition: R&D of $990 million (more than double the closest pure-play peers combined); 10 to 15 launches over two years; more than 80 pipeline products; adjacency moves into retina, cornea and dry age-related macular degeneration; attempted large deals (STAAR at $1.5 billion, LENSAR at $430 million); a stated view that "sustainable incremental revenue growth is more valuable than marginal operating margin gains".
  • Against peers: more ambitious than Cooper (contact lens and fertility focus) and Zeiss (now in profit-improvement mode); comparable to Sonova's chief executive (a CHF 6 billion target by FY2030/31); less disruptive than Johnson & Johnson's current share attack.
  • The gap: the 2023 Capital Markets Day ambitions were not re-set when it became clear they would be missed, which leaves the ambition credible as direction but not as numbers.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D5425th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the strategy is sound in its foundations (renew and monetise the installed base, premiumise contact lenses, build Ocular Health) and has been well executed there. It has not been smart or dynamic enough at the premium IOL edge, where Alcon missed the shift to non-diffractive EDOF and adjustability and is now catching up by licensing. Its adjacency bets are coherent by channel but expensive and unproven.

  • Right and bold: the Unity platform (the largest surgical upgrade in more than a decade; teaching-hospital seeding); the manufacturing investment in contact lenses that let Alcon take share; Tryptyr and Systane preservative-free (prescription and OTC dry eye); the shift to tariff-resilient footprints; the larger buyback at a low multiple.
  • Less smart: (i) premium optics: PowerVision consumed years and $505 million gross before being written off, Vivity was not refreshed before PureSee arrived, and Alcon was slow to recognise RxSight for nearly a year; (ii) bundling and premium pricing without sharing, which invites price-led attacks and FTC scrutiny; (iii) adjacencies such as Neurotech's rare-disease cell therapy, which add complexity and dilution.
  • Robustness across environments: good in a soft-demand environment (annuity, cost levers, balance sheet), weak if a rival's optic advantage persists (no breakthrough lens until the RxSight collaboration matures).

D5637th percentile

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

A8291st percentile

Judgement: very well. Alcon is one of the best-resourced businesses in eye care: built for resilience, with deep R&D, manufacturing, commercial and inventory buffers, and a balance sheet that can absorb shocks.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

D4749th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: Alcon is a good organic allocator, a weak acquirer, and a recently converted, opportunistic repurchaser. On balance it is not yet an enlightened allocator.

Both are dynamic in timing but modest in size (about 1.4% of market capitalisation a year) relative to capacity (1.4x leverage).

Company - Economic strength

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Alcon's economic strength is solid rather than exceptional: a 21% core margin business growing revenue 5% to 6% and core EPS about 10% to 12% a year, with cash conversion at or above 100% of core net income before acquisitions and a lightly geared balance sheet. Its weaknesses are earnings quality (a large and recurring core-to-reported gap, and acquired R&D kept outside the core P&L) and the low returns on acquired capital. Operating leverage is material (40% to 55% underlying drop-through) but has been spent on reinvestment, launches and external costs. The Q3 2026 margin was about 50bp lower year on year once the refund is excluded, and 2027 is pre-guided at under 100bp of net expansion. The mid-term margin path to about 23.5% to 24.5% by 2029 to 2030 is plausible, rests on a few large levers (mix, productivity, SG&A leverage) and is congruent with peers and strategy, but it is two years later than promised. Model implication: base core EBIT margins of about 21.1% (2026), 21.7% (2027), 22.6% (2028) and 23.4% (2029); free cash flow conversion of about 95% to 105% of core net income; capex of 5% to 6% of sales; net debt drifting down unless bolt-ons resume at $300 to $600 million a year; and a reconciliation between core and IFRS earnings that carries recurring restructuring of about $50 to $100 million a year.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C+5773rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Alcon is generating core EPS growth of about 14% in 2026 on about 7% reported revenue growth, but about 3 to 4 points of the EPS growth is non-recurring or non-operating (the tariff refund, currency, the first year of the larger buyback). The durable underlying rate is about 9% to 12% a year on 5% to 6% revenue growth, so consensus 2027 (+11%) is achievable and consensus 2028 (+14.8%) is demanding.

  • History: core EPS of $2.24 (2022), $2.74 (2023, +22%), $3.05 (2024, +11%), $3.07 (2025, +1%); revenue growth on the brokers' basis +8.3%, +5.0% and +4.9% (cc +10%, +6%, +4%). From 2022 to 2025 EPS grew 11.1% a year on 6.0% revenue growth.
  • 2026: 9M core EPS $2.60; the full-year guide (+12% to +14% cc plus about 1.6 points of currency) points to about $3.49 to $3.55, or about +14% to +15% reported. The bridge: revenue +6.9% reported (about 6% cc, +0.9 points currency); core margin from 19.8% to about 21.1% (+6.6% to EBIT); share count from about 495 million to 492 million (+0.6%); interest and tax roughly neutral.
  • Durability, the 2027 bridge (our base): revenue +5% (organic about 4.5% to 5.5% plus Neurotech about 0.5 points, currency neutral); core margin +50 to +90bp (management: under 100bp); share count -1%; interest about $200 to $215 million; tax about 20%. That gives core EPS of about $3.80 to $3.89 (+8.5% to +11% on a $3.50 base). A trap in consensus: the $3.83 FY2027 median shows +11.0% only against the $3.45 FY2026 median, which sits below the guide. Against a likely FY2026 actual of about $3.50, consensus 2027 growth is about +9.5%.
  • 2028: consensus $4.40 (+14.8%) needs core margin +130bp to 23.1% on revenue +5.6%. Our base is +80 to +100bp and about $4.25 to $4.35, about 1% to 3% below consensus.
  • Durability drivers: high (the annuity, buyback, cost programme); medium (margin, which is exposed to dilutive M&A, launch spending and tariffs); low (implantables and equipment).
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D4840th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is moderate scope. Organic reinvestment opportunities with high returns exist (contact lens capacity, the Unity installed base, launch support), but they are bounded by a market growing about 5%. The inorganic outlet, pipeline bolt-ons, has so far earned low or negative returns. The fair multiple is therefore that of a high-quality 10% to 12% EPS compounder with a modest reinvestment runway: about 19x to 22x 24-month-forward earnings, not the 28x post-spin average.

  • High-return organic outlets: contact lens capacity (DSM Flex lines and the Singapore and Grosswallstadt projects; capacity has historically been the gate to share, and Precision7 sales doubled); Unity placements that renew decade-long consumables streams; Ocular Health launch and direct-to-consumer spend for Tryptyr (gated by Medicare Part D, widening in 2027); TruPlus and Vivity Pro launches. Capex runs at about 5% to 6% of sales ($600 to $690 million a year in consensus), R&D at about 9% of sales.
  • Lower-return outlets: acquired pipeline (Aurion, Neurotech, LumiThera, Cylite) at $50 to $600 million a deal, which dilutes core margin for two to three years and carries clinical and commercial risk; the record includes the $402 million PowerVision write-off and the Hydrus royalty.
  • What it means: mid-term revenue growth of 5% to 6% and EPS growth of 10% to 12% with free cash flow of about $1.6 to $2.0 billion a year, about 60% of which will be returned (dividends of about $175 million plus buybacks of about $500 million) and the rest held for bolt-ons. On the dashboard's ten-year bands, the "conservative" level is 22.9x and the "crash" level 20.3x; the five-year 25th percentile is 20.65x and the median 22.64x. A fair range of 19x to 22x against today's 17.1x leaves room for a re-rating once the implantables worry clears, but the historical 28x needed higher growth than this business now offers.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

D4531st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: reported returns are low (ROIC about 5% to 6%) because of the about $22 billion equity base inherited from Novartis at spin-off values. Returns on tangible capital are high (our rough estimate is the high teens to about 20%). Incremental returns are high on organic investment and low on acquisitions. For the investment case, the tangible and organic returns matter for growth quality; the low reported ROIC matters because it signals that acquired growth has not paid.

  • Reported: ROIC of 5.2% (2025) and 5.6% (2026e, Bernstein); peers Cooper 7.8%, Zeiss 6.2%, Smith & Nephew 8.3%; Sonova's ROCE is about 19%. Pre-tax return on capital employed (core EBIT of about $2.04 billion over equity of $22.0 billion plus net debt of about $3.5 billion) is about 8%, or about 6.4% post-tax.
  • Tangible (an estimate; the files do not give net fixed assets): net working capital of about $3.6 billion plus net fixed assets that a decade of $500 to $700 million of annual capex, against depreciation (excluding acquired-intangible amortisation) of about $530 million, suggests are in the order of $4 to $5 billion, giving tangible capital employed of about $8 to $9 billion. Post-tax core EBIT of about $1.65 billion (2025) is then a return of roughly 18% to 21%.
  • Incremental: organic increments (contact lens lines, the Unity base, shared services) earn well above the cost of capital. Acquired increments (Aerie $930 million, Ivantis $479 million, Aurion about $856 million, PowerVision written off, Neurotech $550 million) are mostly not yet earning; about $3 billion of acquisitions since 2022 contributes perhaps $150 to $250 million of revenue in 2026 (Tryptyr, Hydrus, Valeda) at a loss after R&D.
  • Trend: reported ROIC is rising slowly with earnings, and lease-adjusted ROIC was on track to double versus pre-spin levels by 2026 (Redburn).
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

B+7490th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: cash conversion is strong and has stepped up since 2024 (free cash flow at or slightly above core net income), which confirms that core earnings are cash-backed before acquisitions. Working capital is heavy (about 33% of trailing revenue) and is not improving, and receivables and inventory provisions deserve watching.

  • Free cash flow (FactSet: operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex): $481 million (2022), $614 million (2023), $1,474 million (2024), $1,562 million (2025; the company reports $1.70 billion). Against reported net income: 144%, 63%, 145%, 159%. Against core net income (about $1.52 billion in 2025): about 103% (FactSet) to 112% (company). 9M 2026: operating cash flow $1.689 billion and free cash flow $1.345 billion against 9M core net income of about $1.28 billion, about 105%.
  • What drove the 2024 step-up: capex normalised after the Novartis separation (fixed-asset capex from $636 to $700 million in 2021 to 2023, to $473 to $543 million in 2024 to 2025), and operating cash flow rose from $1.39 billion to $2.08 billion.
  • Consensus: free cash flow of $1,582 million (2026), $1,733 million (2027) and $2,030 million (2028), against adjusted net income of $1,688, $1,850 and $2,107 million, which is conversion of about 94% to 96%. That is below the current run rate, so it is not a stretch.
  • Working capital: receivables plus inventories less payables of $3.3 to $3.6 billion, steady at 32.6% to 34.8% of trailing revenue since 2024. In 2025 receivables rose $206 million (faster than sales), inventories rose $123 million, inventory provisions were $206 million (as in 2023), and payables rose $153 million.
  • Leakages outside FactSet's free cash flow: intangible capex of about $100 to $150 million a year (total capex including intangibles was $669 million in 2024, $641 million in 2025 and $491 million in 9M 2026); leases; and acquisitions ($648 million in 2025). "Owner" free cash flow after typical bolt-on spend is about 75% to 85% of core net income.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

B6579th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: margins are high and rising, but they are rising more slowly than management promised in 2023 and still trail the best peers. Gross margin is strong and improving on productivity and mix; operating margin expansion is being held back by reinvestment (R&D at the top of its range, launch costs, acquired pipeline) and external costs (tariffs, currency).

  • Gross: core gross margin of 62.5% to 62.9% in 2024 to 2025; 64.7% in Q2 2026 (+250bp, helped by a $15 million licensee windfall). On FactSet's basis (which includes amortisation), 55.2% in 2025, 56.4% in Q1 2026, and 59.3% in Q2 and Q3 2026 (from 53.5% and 55.8%).
  • Core operating margin: 17.2% (2019), 18.2% (2022), 19.7% (2023), 20.6% (2024), 19.8% (2025, -80bp on currency of about -130bp, tariffs and Aurion R&D of about -80bp); Q1 2026 21.2%, Q2 20.6%, Q3 21.8% (including the $58 million refund, about +210bp in the quarter); FY2026 guided at +100 to +150bp cc, about 21.0% to 21.3%. Consensus core EBIT margin: 21.1% (2026), 21.8% (2027), 23.1% (2028), 23.9% (2029). Consensus EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis: 25.7% (2025), then 26.1%, 27.1%, 28.4% and 29.5%.
  • Peers: Cooper's operating margin is about 250bp higher; Sonova's normalised EBITA is 23.7% (25.4% in H2); Smith & Nephew's trading margin is 19.7%; Carl Zeiss Meditec is in the high single digits.
  • The Q3 2026 margin was flattered: the $58 million refund is about 210bp of Q3 revenue, while core margin rose only 160bp. Without the refund, Q3 core margin was about 19.7%, roughly 50bp lower year on year, despite +5% revenue growth and the savings programme. That is the clearest evidence that launch spending, the implantables mix and input inflation are absorbing operating leverage, and it supports management's under-100bp signal for 2027.
  • Trend: about +70bp a year underlying from 2019 to 2024, a one-year reversal in 2025, +130bp in 2026 (of which about 25bp is net refund and 10bp currency), and under 100bp net in 2027. The 2028 consensus step (+130bp) assumes that Neurotech dilution fades, implantables recover and the $130 million savings fully land.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D2529th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the core earnings are cash-backed but not clean. The core-to-reported gap is very large; most of it is legitimate non-cash amortisation inherited at the spin-off, but a growing, recurring share consists of "one-offs" (restructuring, impairments, discontinuations) and of R&D bought through acquisitions that never passes through the core income statement. Tax does not leak; if anything, the reported tax rate runs below the core rate.

  • Size of the gap: FY2025 core EPS of $3.07 against IFRS basic EPS of $1.99 (35% lower); 9M 2026 core EPS of $2.60 against IFRS basic EPS of about $0.93 (including the $402 million PowerVision impairment). Q1 2026 core adjustments were $277 million (against $43 million in Q1 2025); the 2025 exclusions totalled $1.1 billion. Q3 2026 IFRS operating margin was 14.0% against a core margin of 21.8%.
  • Legitimate and non-cash: amortisation of acquired intangibles of about $800 million a year (FactSet's total depreciation and amortisation of $1,335 million in 2025 against consensus depreciation of about $530 million that excludes it). Morgan Stanley notes that amortisation equalled nearly all of five-year adjusted net income after the spin.
  • Recurring "one-offs" that flatter core: restructuring (the programme grew from $150 million to $230 million; $121 million in H1 2026); impairments ($402 million in 2026, $45 million in 2025); product discontinuation charges ($44 million in 2025); exclusions apply above a $10 million threshold. Recurring inventory provisions ($206 million in 2025 and 2023) appear to sit within core.
  • R&D outside core: acquired in-process R&D is capitalised (Aurion's purchase allocated $820 million to IPR&D Neurotech $550 million; Aerie $930 million), so the pipeline-building that rivals expense flows through investing cash flow rather than the core P&L. Total "innovation spend" (internal R&D of about $1 billion plus about $0.5 billion a year of acquired pipeline on average since 2022) is about 13% to 15% of sales, against the 8% to 9% shown in core.
  • Timing items in core: the $58 million tariff refund (Q3 2026) is included in core; about $80 million of capitalised tariffs flow through cost of goods with a lag.
  • Tax: the core effective tax rate is about 20% (2026 guide); the reported rate was 15.3% (2025), 18.8% (2024) and 21.3% (Q1 2026). No hidden leakage is evident in the files, but Swiss and global minimum-tax changes are not covered by them.
  • Cash check: free cash flow of $1.56 to $1.70 billion against core net income of about $1.52 billion in 2025 confirms that core earnings are cash-backed before acquisitions. After the average bolt-on spend, "owner earnings" are about 75% to 85% of core net income.
  • Governance angle: executive pay is set on core diluted EPS, core operating income and free cash flow, which gives an incentive to keep items out of core. Morgan Stanley ranks Alcon's reporting quality in the bottom tier.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

B7279th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is low and very comfortable. It barely amplifies EPS or risk, and it leaves material untapped capacity for buybacks or acquisitions. On the environment digest's Fragility test, Alcon is among the least fragile names in the sector.

  • Levels: net debt of $3.53 billion at Q3 2026 on FactSet's definition (including leases; the company's own measure was $3.25 billion at 30 June 2026); 1.41x trailing EBITDA (FactSet), 1.4x to 1.47x on company and Bernstein figures for 2025, projected at about 0.9x by 2027 absent larger deals. Gross debt of $5.2 to $5.3 billion with an 8.8-year average maturity, 98% fixed; cash of $1.8 billion; a $1.32 billion undrawn revolving facility.
  • Earnings sensitivity: net interest of about $200 to $217 million (consensus implies $217 million in 2026, $213 million in 2027 and $186 million in 2028) is about 9% of core EBIT; interest cover is about 11x. Because 98% of debt is fixed, a 100bp rate rise barely moves interest cost. Leverage adds little to EPS growth and little to its volatility.
  • Capacity: at 2.5x EBITDA, a level many medtech peers carry (Bausch + Lomb and Demant are well above it), Alcon could add about $3 billion of debt, enough for roughly 8% of its share capital or a mid-sized acquisition, without stressing credit.
  • Peers: Carl Zeiss Meditec and Smith & Nephew at 1.8x, EssilorLuxottica at 2.0x, Sonova at 1.0x to 1.1x.
  • One unusual covenant link: a material breach of the Lifecore supply agreement triggers a default under Alcon's credit facility. It is small in substance but unusual.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C+4967th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price adds about +1.0% to +1.5% of group revenue a year, almost all of it in United States contact lenses and Ocular Health, and almost all of it is consumed by cost inflation, so it protects the margin rather than expanding it. Price is a net negative in premium IOLs (through rebates and bundles, not list cuts), in China (procurement) and in equipment (the Unity CS mix). The near-term risk is specific and dated: the two United States contact lens price increases of H1 2026 are lapped in H1 2027, so unless they are repeated, contact lens growth slows by 1 to 2 points in H2 2027. We assume a smaller repeat. The longer-term risk is that premium IOL price has to fall openly if Vivity Pro does not stop the share loss.

Price by line (our base, annual contribution to each line's growth):

Line (share of 2026 sales) 2027 2028 to 2030 Evidence
Contact lenses (26%) +2.5% (United States +3% to +4%; international 0% to +1%) +2% to +2.5% About 4 points of the 5% Q2 2026 growth was price (chief executive, 11-Aug-26); United States +8% on two increases that "stuck exceptionally well" (Deutsche Bank, 17-Aug-26); international growth only 1% to 2% on online and private-label transparency (Bernstein, 12-Nov-26); industry growth 4% to 5% of which about 3 points price (Q3 2026 call)
Ocular Health (18%) +1% to +2% (Systane premium formats; Tryptyr gross-to-net worsens as Medicare Part D volume arrives) +1.5% Systane preservative-free volumes doubled in Q3 2026; Bausch + Lomb walked away from CVS coverage for Xiidra on margin
Consumables (29%) +0.5% to +1% +0.5% to +1% Premium Custom Packs fund Unity placements; PE-owned ASCs (about 20% of United States cataract volume) push vendor-agnostic buying
Implantables (16%) -1% to -2% (premium average selling price, net of rebates); China IOLs -30% to -40% on the next procurement round -0.5% to -1% Alcon holds list price on Vivity and PanOptix Pro and uses volume rebates tied to its equipment base; PureSee priced about $150 (15%) below Vivity in Europe; Bausch + Lomb about $100 cheaper
Equipment (10%) -5% to -8% average selling price (mix to Unity CS) flat Management: units "stable", mix shift to the lower-priced standalone Unity CS (11-Nov-26)
Group about +1.0% about +1.2% to +1.5%

How price reaches the margin: price drops through at close to 100%, so +1.0% to +1.5% of revenue is about $115 to $170 million a year of gross profit, equivalent to 100 to 140bp of margin before costs. Against it: wage inflation on about $4.6 billion of operating expenses (about 3%, $140 million), materials inflation (chips, resins), tariffs. The net is roughly zero: price funds inflation, and the margin expansion in our base comes from volume leverage, mix and savings. This is the key difference from the hearing aid makers, where a launch premium of about 10% (Sonova's Infinio Sphere) lifted margins directly; Alcon's patient-paid premium tier (premium IOLs) is precisely where it is not currently earning price.

Why the United States contact lens price is sustainable but not repeatable at the 2026 pace: the category is an oligopoly (top four about 90%) whose players describe it as "highly civilised"; optometrists are gatekeepers who compete on innovation rather than price; wearers rarely trade down from daily lenses (Morgan Stanley optometry checks, March 2026); Cooper guides 1% to 3% a year and did not cut price even through destocking. But two increases in one half-year were exceptional (they followed tariffs and the dollar), private-label daily lenses (Cooper's MyDay private label) are gaining among value-conscious practices, and the international pattern (Chinese online share from 46% to 80% of contact lens sales between 2018 and 2023) is the warning of what transparency does. We model one United States increase in 2027 rather than two: contact lens growth of about +5% in H1 2027 and about +4.5% in H2 2027, then +5.5% a year from 2028 as mix (daily silicone hydrogels, Precision7, Total30 multifocal toric) takes over from price.

Premium IOLs: will price hold through 2027 to 2028? The evidence says Alcon is defending average selling price through bundles and volume rebates (clinics risk losing a substantial part of their quarterly rebate if Alcon lens use drops by even 10%), not list cuts. That preserves reported price but is the economic equivalent of price erosion: share has fallen instead. If Vivity Pro restores parity, list prices can hold; if it does not, the next step is visible discounting, because PE-owned ASCs and cash-strapped clinics (Medicare fees -11% in 2026, -4% proposed for 2027) have every incentive to push. We model premium IOL net price at -1% to -2% a year in 2027 and 2028, which costs about $15 to $30 million of revenue a year (about 0.15 to 0.25 points of group growth), and treat open list-price cuts as a bear-case trigger, not a base case.

China: the next volume-based procurement round (2026 to 2027) is expected to bring 30% to 40% price headwinds across premium and monofocal IOLs (Morgan Stanley, 2-Sep-26), with domestic makers (EyeBright, Vision Pro) entering the premium tier. China is 5% to 6% of group sales; IOLs are a small part of that. We take about -$30 to -$40 million of revenue in H2 2027 to 2028, partly offset by volume. Cochlear's experience (public-hospital list price -75%, units +16%, blended price -37%, regional revenue -27%) shows volume rarely compensates.

Tariff pass-through: management admitted "very little pricing traction" specifically for tariffs (January 2026) and absorbed 2025's $91 million of tariff charges mainly through currency and cost control. We do not assume price recovers the 2027 tariff headwind.

The margin effect in one sentence: price contributes about +1 point of revenue growth and close to zero net margin; a failure of United States contact lens price to repeat would cost about 0.5 points of group growth and about 30 to 40bp of margin in 2027, which is the most likely way our 2027 margin could fall short apart from implantables.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7555P-0152
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C5561st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: operating leverage is material but was largely absorbed by reinvestment and external costs in 2025 to 2026. Drop-through on incremental revenue is about 40% to 55% underlying, so each point of revenue growth is worth about 2% to 2.5% of core EBIT and core EPS. The resulting range of outcomes for 2027 is moderate: core EPS of about $3.60 to $4.05 around a base of about $3.85.

  • Cost structure: core cost of goods of about 35% to 37% of sales (core gross margin 63% to 65%), with manufacturing overhead and depreciation largely fixed (16 to 17 plants, about 90% in-house). Opex of about 42% to 43% of sales: SG&A about 33% (a 4,100-strong sales force, shared services, marketing) and R&D about 9%, mostly semi-fixed. The Business Foundations report and Bernstein describe "highly fixed manufacturing and administrative cost structures" and "very high incremental contribution margins".
  • Evidence of drop-through: from 2022 to 2024 core EBIT rose about $450 million on about $1.18 billion of extra revenue (about 38% incremental). In 2025 it rose only about $20 million on $480 million (about 4%, with currency at about -130bp, tariffs, about -80bp from Aurion, and launch costs). In 2026e it rises about $280 million on about $700 million (about 40%, including the refund and currency). Management's 2027 "underlying leverage of 150 to 200bp" at about 5% growth implies underlying incremental margins of about 45% to 55% before the headwinds.
  • Sensitivity: one point of revenue (about $110 million) at 40% to 55% drop-through is $45 to $60 million of core EBIT, or 2% to 2.6% of core EBIT and about $0.07 to $0.10 of core EPS. Deutsche Bank's figure of $0.04 per point uses the average margin and understates it. Mix matters: a point of implantables (about $18 million) carries a higher margin than a point of equipment. A 1% dollar move is worth $52 million of sales and $29 million of operating income (56% drop-through, because costs in francs and dollars exceed revenues in those currencies).
  • 2027 range: bear case revenue +3.5% with margin flat (implantables -2%, equipment -5%, launch costs) gives EPS of about $3.62; base case +5% and +60bp gives about $3.85; bull case +7% and +150bp (Vivity Pro succeeds, surgeon capacity eases, savings over-deliver) gives about $4.05.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D5247th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Alcon's operating leverage is real and roughly constant at about 40% to 50% drop-through on incremental revenue before reinvestment, but its history shows that what reaches the core margin is decided by management's reinvestment rate, not by the cost structure. Over the last six years about half of the gross leverage was spent on R&D, launches and acquired pipelines. We expect the same over the next four years: gross leverage of about 150bp a year at 5% growth, of which about 60 to 100bp a year reaches the core margin. That gives core EBIT margins of 21.0% (2026), 21.6% (2027), 22.6% (2028), 23.3% (2029) and 23.9% (2030), against 17.2% (2019), 19.7% (2023), 20.6% (2024) and 19.8% (2025). The pattern is the same as the past; the level is higher, and the mid-20s margin promised for 2027 arrives in about 2030.

What the cost structure is. Core cost of goods is about 35% to 37% of sales (core gross margin 63% to 66%; 65.7% in Q3 2026 including the refund), with plant overhead and depreciation largely fixed: about 90% of products are made in-house in 16 to 17 plants. Operating expenses are about 42% to 44% of sales, of which SG&A is about 33% (a 4,100-strong sales force, shared services, marketing) and R&D about 9% (held at the top of the 7% to 9% range). Management said in January 2021 that two-thirds of long-term margin expansion comes from fixed-cost leverage on volume. The company does not disclose the fixed and variable split; the evidence is the drop-through record.

The record, which is the best evidence of how leverage behaves (core EBIT on extra revenue; Bernstein, 12-Nov-26):

Year Revenue ($m) Core EBIT ($m) Core margin Drop-through on extra revenue
2019 7,362 1,265 17.2% -
2020 6,763 789 11.7% 79% (on the way down)
2021 8,222 1,443 17.6% 45%
2022 8,654 1,571 18.2% 30% (inflation)
2023 9,370 1,849 19.7% 39%
2024 9,836 2,027 20.6% 38%
2025 10,319 2,039 19.8% 2% (currency -130bp, tariffs, Aurion R&D -80bp, launches)
2026 (our base) 11,033 2,316 21.0% 39% (including the refund and currency)
2027 (our base) 11,585 2,502 21.6% 34%
2028 (our base) 12,199 2,757 22.6% 42%

Three patterns matter. (1) In "normal" years drop-through clusters at 38% to 45% (2021, 2023, 2024, 2026), equivalent to 60 to 90bp of margin expansion at 5% to 8% growth. (2) It collapses to near zero when external costs and reinvestment coincide (2022: inflation; 2025: currency, tariffs, Aurion). Those years are not rare: two of the last six. (3) It is symmetric on the way down (79% in 2020): the cost base does not flex quickly, which is why a growth shortfall hurts more than a cost overrun.

How gross leverage becomes net margin in 2026 and 2027 (the critical bridge).

  • 2026: the guide of +100 to +150bp cc (plus about 10bp currency) contains the $58 million refund (about 53bp of sales) less about $40 million reinvested in Q4 (about 36bp), so a net refund effect of about +17bp. Underlying expansion at the midpoint is therefore about +100 to +110bp on about 6% cc growth with about $50 million of savings. But it is uneven: Q3 2026 core margin without the refund was about 19.7%, about 50bp below Q3 2025 (20.2%), on +5% cc growth. The year's underlying leverage was front-loaded into H1 (the Unity equipment wave and Ocular Health at +12%) and faded in Q3 as growth slowed and launch spending continued. That is the most recent and most relevant data point, and it is the reason we take the low end of management's 2027 range.
  • 2027: management's framework (10 to 11 November 2026) is underlying leverage of 150 to 200bp, net expansion "under 100bp". Bernstein (12-Nov-26) builds +175bp underlying, less 50 to 55bp for the refund's non-recurrence, 10 to 15bp for a 2026 licensee milestone that does not recur, about 20bp for memory chip and resin inflation, about 10bp for currency and about 20bp for Neurotech dilution, giving about +60bp to 21.8%. Redburn (11-Nov-26) puts the refund and milestone together at $75 million (about 70bp) and cut its 2027 EBIT to $2,005 million. Our base is +60bp to 21.6%: underlying 150bp (the low end, because growth is at the low end of 6% to 8%, and because the H2 2026 run rate was weaker than H1), the same offsets as Bernstein, and a partial (about 15 to 20bp) cushion from the non-recurrence of part of the Q4 2026 reinvestment, which Bernstein's bridge does not credit.
  • The swing factor nobody quantifies: if the Q4 2026 reinvestment was one-off spending (launch and R&D outlays timed to use the refund), 2027 loses the refund but also loses the spending, and the net headwind is only about 15bp rather than 50 to 55bp. If it was the first quarter of a permanently higher R&D and launch run rate, the full 50 to 55bp applies. Management has not said. This one disclosure is worth about $0.05 to $0.08 of 2027 EPS (about 1.5% to 2%). The Q4 2026 results and FY2027 guide (2 March 2027) should reveal it.

2028 to 2030: why +100bp, +70bp, +60bp rather than consensus's +130bp in 2028. In 2028 the drags that cap 2027 fade (Neurotech dilution +10 to +20bp as ENCELTO scales; the refund comparison is gone), implantables mix turns from a drag to a small help (+10 to +20bp if Vivity Pro stabilises share), and gross leverage at 5.3% growth gives about 120 to 150bp. Against that: AURN001 pre-launch spending (approval targeted late 2028; peak above $500 million per Redburn), the Hydrus royalty to Sight Sciences until late 2028, recurring tariffs of $80 to $100 million gross, continued bolt-on dilution of 20 to 40bp per deal in its first two years, and R&D held at about 9% of sales. Consensus at +130bp in 2028 needs all of: full $130 million of savings in the run rate, implantables recovery, Neurotech break-even, no new dilutive deal and no tariff escalation. On the record, Alcon has delivered a year of +130bp or more only coming out of a downturn (2021, 2023) or with a one-off (2026). We therefore give 2028 +100bp, which is still above the 2019 to 2024 average of about +70bp a year.

How leverage shapes the main line items:

  • Revenue: leverage does not shape revenue, but it makes revenue the dominant sensitivity. One point of revenue growth (about $115 million in 2027) at 40% to 50% drop-through is $45 to $58 million of core EBIT, or about 2% of core EBIT and $0.07 to $0.10 of core EPS. Mix matters: a point of implantables (about $18 million) carries a higher contribution than a point of equipment; the 2027 shift away from equipment (lower margin) toward consumables and Ocular Health is a small positive for gross margin even as it slows revenue.
  • Gross margin: roughly flat to +50bp in 2027 (price, the Unity consumables cycle, lower contact lens unit costs as the Singapore and Grosswallstadt lines fill, against tariffs, chips, resins and the lower-priced Unity CS). New contact lens lines take 24 to 36 months to fill, and Alcon has suffered 240bp of gross margin under-absorption in a past ramp; the lines are an absorption headwind in 2027 and a tailwind from 2028.
  • Operating expenses: the main source of net expansion: SG&A leverage on a semi-fixed commercial base plus the savings programme. R&D grows with sales.
  • EBITDA margin (our input): 26.4%, 27.1%, 28.1%, 28.8%, 29.4% (core EBIT plus about 5.5% depreciation), against 25.7% in 2025 on the brokers' basis.

The width of the cone for 2027 created by leverage: bear case revenue +3.5% with margin flat (implantables -2%, equipment -5%, launch spending and the full refund headwind) gives core EPS of about $3.60 (-5% against our base); bull case revenue +6.5% and +120bp (Vivity Pro wins back share, the reinvestment proves one-off, inflation eases) gives about $4.00 (+5%). The skew is slightly to the downside in H1 2027 (equipment comparisons of +23% and +25% cc, implantables still under sampling pressure) and balanced for the full year.

Against peers: Cooper sustains about 250bp higher operating margin in contact lenses on scale (and lifted its own by 500bp in a manufacturing modernisation); Sonova expanded core EBIT margin by 250bp to 20.9% in its H1 FY2026/27 on volume and cost discipline; Carl Zeiss Meditec shows the reverse case, with adjusted EBITA margin down to about 8% as volume fell on a fixed base. Alcon sits between: more leverage than Zeiss because of its consumables annuity, less margin delivery than Cooper or Sonova because it reinvests more.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7672P-0258
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?B7279th percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C+5872nd percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is low, falling and almost irrelevant to EPS growth, except through the buyback it funds. The balance sheet de-levers mechanically from about 1.3x EBITDA at end-2026 (after the $550 million Neurotech payment) to about 0.6x by end-2028 and close to zero by 2030, even with $400 million a year of bolt-ons and buybacks stepping up to $800 to $900 million a year. That creates $4 to $5 billion of unused debt capacity by 2028 at 2x EBITDA. How that capacity is used is a bigger EPS question than interest cost: spent on buybacks at about 18x earnings it would add about 8% to 10% to EPS; spent on a STAAR-sized acquisition it would dilute EPS by 2% to 4% for two to three years. Management's stated order (organic investment, then bolt-ons, then returns) makes the second more likely than the first. Our base assumes neither.

The starting point (30 September 2026): gross debt $5.34 billion (FactSet, including leases), cash $1.81 billion, net debt $3.53 billion (1.41x trailing FactSet EBITDA); the company's own net debt measure excludes leases ($3.25 billion at 30 June 2026). 98% of financial debt is at fixed rates with an 8.8-year average maturity; the $1.32 billion revolving facility is undrawn. In Q4 2026 the $550 million Neurotech upfront payment and about $190 million of buybacks are paid against about $490 million of free cash flow, so net debt ends 2026 at about $3.8 billion, about 1.3x core EBITDA.

Our net debt path (base, USD million):

2026 2027 2028 2029 2030
Free cash flow (after capex including intangibles and leases) 1,610 1,740 1,980 2,180 2,370
Dividends -175 -190 -210 -225 -245
Buybacks -780 -500 -500 -800 -900
Acquisitions -560 -350 -400 -400 -400
Net debt at year end (approx.) 3,790 3,090 2,220 1,460 640
Net debt to core EBITDA 1.3x 1.0x 0.65x 0.4x 0.15x

How it shapes EPS:

  • Interest: net financial items of about -$220 million in 2026 (non-operating expenses guided at $210 to $230 million in November 2026, raised by higher interest on lease liabilities and currency losses), -$215 million (2027), -$205 million (2028), -$200 million (2029), -$195 million (2030). With 98% fixed debt, rates barely matter; the decline comes from cash build. Interest is about 8% to 9% of core EBIT, falling to about 6%. It adds about 1 point a year to EPS growth against EBIT growth (PBT +9.1% on EBIT +8.0% in 2027; +11.6% on +10.2% in 2028), because a flat interest bill is spread over rising EBIT. Consensus implies $217 million, $213 million and $186 million, so it is slightly more optimistic than us for 2028 (it may assume more cash interest income).
  • The buyback (the only financial lever with real EPS effect): about $1.0 billion remains of the $1.5 billion three-year programme started in May 2026 (more than one-third completed per the CFO in November 2026). We assume $500 million a year in 2027 and 2028 (completing the programme a few months early) and a successor programme of $800 million (2029) and $900 million (2030). Net of equity awards (grants exceeded vesting by 400,000 shares in Q1 2026; we assume about 3 million shares a year issued to employees), the weighted diluted share count goes from about 487 million (2026) to about 482 million (2027), 478 million (2028), 474 million (2029) and 467 million (2030), about -0.8% to -1.3% a year. That is about +1 point of EPS growth a year, about one-tenth of the total.
  • Leverage does not amplify EPS volatility: a 10% fall in core EBIT lowers EPS by about 11%; financial gearing adds only about 1 point of amplification.

How it shapes the balance sheet: equity of about $22 billion (consensus $21.9 billion at end-2026, rising to $23.5 billion by 2028) is dominated by goodwill and intangibles inherited from the Novartis spin-off; reported ROIC stays at 5% to 7% because of that base, while returns on tangible capital stay in the high teens. Net working capital stays at about 33% of revenue (32.6% to 34.8% since 2024; 33.2% at Q3 2026), absorbing about $180 to $210 million of cash a year as revenue grows. The one unusual covenant link (a material breach of the Lifecore supply agreement triggers a default under Alcon's credit facility) is small in substance.

The insight for the Consumer: the de-leveraging is itself a signal about what happens next. Alcon generates about $1.0 to $1.5 billion a year of cash after dividends and bolt-ons from 2027. A management that keeps leverage near 1.4x (its 2025 to 2026 level) would need to spend about $2 billion more by 2028 than our base. Three outcomes are possible, and each is visible in advance: (a) a larger buyback at the March 2027 results or a Capital Markets Day (upside to EPS of 2% to 4% a year from 2028); (b) a larger acquisition (the STAAR bid at $1.5 billion and the LENSAR attempt show the appetite; management "continues to actively evaluate" deals of $50 to $500 million per Deutsche Bank); (c) cash simply builds, which lowers ROE and keeps interest falling. Our base sits between (a) and (c). Pershing Square's presence as a holder that argues for 25% margins makes (a) more likely than it was a year ago, but nothing in the files shows management moving off "organic first".

Peers: Carl Zeiss Meditec and Smith & Nephew carry about 1.8x, EssilorLuxottica 2.0x, Sonova 1.0x to 1.1x; Bausch + Lomb and Demant are well above 2.5x. On the environment digest's Fragility row Alcon is among the least fragile names in the sector.

Cost-out scope NTM
