I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

D4537th percentile

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: guidance is most likely to be met. The first formal 2027 guide should bracket where the updated brokers already are, and the most likely change during 2027 is a trim to the sales range with EPS held. A raise needs an operating surprise that the first half of 2027 is poorly placed to deliver, because 2027 has none of the non-operating windfalls (share count, tariff refund, currency) that produced both of 2026's raises. The 2028 guide, in about March 2028, is where guidance is most likely to come in below today's published consensus. The stale mid-term targets will be reduced in substance on 2 March 2027 and, about as likely as not, formally within 18 months. Guidance is realistic, not conservative. It holds no hidden cushion for the case, and little threat to it.

The events, and our probabilities:

Event Raised or above Met or in line Reduced or below What decides it
2-Mar-27: the FY2026 result against the November guide (sales about 6% cc: met, 90%; margin +100 to +150bp: met in the lower half, 80%) EPS above $3.55: about 5% EPS within $3.49 to $3.55, most likely in the lower half: about 60% EPS a cent or two below $3.49: about 35% The Q4 margin with about $40 million reinvested (about 20.8% is needed for the low end; the second set has 20.4%); tax within 19% to 20%
2-Mar-27: the formal FY2027 guide, against the updated brokers (revenue +5.8%, EPS $3.84) Midpoint more than 1% above: 10% Midpoint within 1%: 55% Midpoint more than 1% below: 35% Whether the Q4 reinvestment recurs; China; the dollar on the day; how much room management wants
May and August 2027: the FY2027 EPS guide Raised: 10% Held: 80% Cut: 10% First-half growth of about 4%; United States implantables; equipment against +23% and +25%. The sales range is trimmed, or narrowed to its lower half, by August with a probability of about 25%
By November 2027, cumulatively for the year: the FY2027 EPS guide Raised at some point: 20% Held or narrowed: 55% Cut at some point: 25% The second-half recovery; Medicare coverage for Tryptyr; the margin after lapping the refund. The sales range is lowered, or narrowed to its lower half, at some point in the year with a probability of about 50%
About March 2028: the FY2028 guide, against the updated brokers ($4.32 to $4.33) Midpoint more than 1% above: 10% Midpoint within 1%: 45% Midpoint more than 1% below: 45% Whether management frames 2028 EPS growth at about +10% to +13%. The probability that the midpoint is below today's FactSet median ($4.40) is 60% or more

What the FY2027 guide will probably say, and why "in line" is the most likely outcome. Management has already said growth will be at the low end of 6% to 8% including Neurotech, and net margin expansion under 100bp. The form of the 2026 guide suggests sales growth of +5% to +7% cc, margin expansion of roughly +40 to +90bp and core EPS growth of roughly +8% to +11% cc. On a 2026 base of about $3.49, that is about $3.77 to $3.87 before currency, a midpoint of about $3.82 against the updated brokers' $3.84 and our $3.80. Giving the frame four months early had a purpose: it walked consensus to where the guide will be, so that the guide is met on the day. Redburn "welcomed the conservative tone". After two cuts in 2025, and with pay set on core EPS, management's interest is in a guide it can hold. The reason it could be set lower (35%) is that the Q4 report will itself be weak in the lines the market watches, and a management that wants to rebuild a record of raising has an incentive to start low.

Why a raise is less likely in 2027 than 2026's record suggests. The 2026 raises came in May (the share count, from the new buyback) and August (the tariff refund). The operating guide was met at its original midpoint, not beaten, and was then trimmed in November. In 2027 the buyback is already in the base, there is no refund, and currency is guided as neutral. A raise would have to be earned in the business, in a year whose first half carries the equipment comparison, continued sampling, China's price cuts and no Medicare coverage for Tryptyr.

Why a trim to sales is as likely as not. In each of 2024, 2025 and 2026 the sales or margin guide was lowered or trimmed during the year. Our base for 2027 (+5.0% reported, about +4.6% organic plus Neurotech) sits at or just below the bottom of a +5% to +7% range, and the updated brokers (+5.8%) sit near its middle. The pattern to expect is the one seen in 2024 and in November 2026: sales narrowed to the lower half, EPS held by the cost line.

What would drive each outcome in 2027:

  • Raised: United States implantables growth turns positive by the Q2 report (Vivity Pro stops the share loss); the Q4 2026 reinvestment proves to be one-off (worth 20 to 35bp of margin); a weaker dollar (each 1% is about 1.25% of EPS); a larger buyback; chip and resin costs easing.
  • Met: the annuity lines at about +5%; the cost line flexed; easy comparisons in the second half.
  • Reduced: implantables negative through the first half, China included; equipment down by more than 5% in the first half; no repeat of the contact lens price increase; Tryptyr slow before Medicare coverage; Section 301 tariffs; a stronger dollar.

Why the 2028 guide is the one most likely to disappoint the published consensus. Management's language for a normal year is underlying leverage of 150 to 200bp less whatever is being absorbed, and double-digit EPS growth. In 2028 the things being absorbed are launch costs for the corneal cell therapy (approval targeted for late 2028), the last year of the Hydrus royalty and any new bolt-on acquisitions. A frame of +10% to +13% EPS growth on a 2027 base of $3.80 to $3.84 has a midpoint of about $4.24 to $4.28: our base, about 3% below the FactSet median and 1% to 2% below the updated brokers. The first indication will come as early as November 2027, if management again gives a preliminary frame with its Q3 results.

Secondarily: the mid-term targets (18 months to three years out).

  • "Mid-20s core margin" and "$12 billion of sales" by 2027 (set at the 2023 capital markets day; a further capital markets day was held in March 2025, and the targets have not been formally withdrawn). The 2027 guide will reduce them in substance: a margin of about 21.5% to 21.8% and sales of about $11.6 billion. We put that at 95%. It is fully expected: consensus has 21.8% for 2027.
  • A formal new mid-term framework within 18 months (a capital markets day, or with results): about 45%. If it comes, the likely form is mid-single-digit growth or a little more, 50 to 100bp of margin a year, and the mid-20s by about 2030. That would be a reduction against the old targets, in line with our base and with consensus (23.1% for 2028, 23.9% for 2029), and below the 25% or more that Pershing Square argues for.
  • The 6% to 8% growth target: kept in words until a formal reset; lowered within 18 months with a probability of about 35%.
  • Any mid-term target raised: under 5%.
  • The precedent: Coloplast reset its 2030 targets in November 2026 (growth from 7%-8% to 5%-7%, and a lower margin target) after its share price had halved over two years; brokers called the reset needed. A reset by Alcon would likewise be a reduction on the headline and neutral for estimates. It would remove a credibility overhang. It would also confirm in management's own words that this is a 5% to 6% grower, which bears on the multiple the market will pay.

What this means for the gap between our assessment and guidance. There is almost none in the numbers. Guidance already says what we think: growth at the low end, net margin expansion under 100bp, EPS growth of about 10%. The memo's test of "conservative guidance, and a record of raising it" is not met: the guide is realistic, the only recent raises were non-operating, and the base rate favours a trim to sales. For a case built on upgrades this is unfavourable. For a case built on delivery it is neutral.

Our base revenue ($ million) 11,033 11,585 12,199

Four observations that the medians hide:

  1. Two mechanical moves are coming that are not news. The FY2026 median should rise about 1% (toward $3.49) and the FY2028 median should fall 1.5% to 2% (toward $4.32 to $4.35) simply as older estimates are refreshed. The second will register as negative revisions.
  2. The disagreement is in 2028, not 2027. Thirteen of the fourteen updated estimates for 2027 lie within $3.79 to $3.92, a spread of 3.4%. For 2028 the spread is 12%. The typical updated broker has 2028 EPS growth of about 12.5%, as we do; four of twelve have 13% or more, and those are the estimates that will fall as management's 2028 frame emerges.
  3. Revenue is where the updated brokers are furthest above us: +5.8% growth for 2027 against our +5.0%, a difference of 0.7% in the level. The brokers have taken management's "low end of 6% to 8%" at its word. Alcon's revenue has come in below consensus in seven of the last nine quarters.
  4. By line, consensus looks too high in equipment and too low in Ocular Health. Consensus equipment for 2027 ($1,170 million) is growth of about 7% on our reading of the base (D-01), against management's "units stable" and a console mix moving to about half the price: an excess of roughly $60 to $70 million. Consensus Ocular Health ($2,147 million) is about $80 million below our base, probably because Neurotech is not in every model. The two roughly cancel in revenue. They do not cancel in the quarters: the equipment shortfall arrives in the first half and ENCELTO in the second.

The recent movement. FY2027 EPS is down 1.1% over one month, 2.6% over three and 3.0% over twelve; FY2028 is down 0.8%, 2.4% and 1.8%. The share of buy ratings is 65%, down 6 points in three months and 12 in six. The dashboard's revision-momentum score is -10 and its pinned rating is F. The median target ($86) is 18% above the share price.

The likely path, event by event:

Window FY2027 EPS consensus FY2028 EPS consensus Breadth of revisions
To 2 March 2027 Flat to -0.5% Median -1% to -2%, mechanical Negative
At the guide (2 March 2027) Held within 1%: 50%. Cut 1% to 2%: 40%. Raised: 10% Cut 1% to 2%: 50%. Held: 45% Negative to neutral; first-half quarters lowered
May and August 2027 Held: 60%. Cut 1% to 2%: 30%. Raised: 10% Trimmed 0% to 1% at each Negative, turning neutral by August
November 2027 to May 2028 FY2027 lands within 2% of today's $3.84: 75% Settles at about $4.20 to $4.30 as the 2028 frame and guide arrive Neutral; positive only if United States implantables are growing

Cumulatively over the 18 months:

  • FY2027 EPS: upgraded by more than 1%: 15%. Held between -2% and +1%: 55%. Cut by more than 2%: 30%.
  • FY2028 EPS against the FactSet median ($4.40): upgraded: 10%. Held within 1.5%: 30%. Cut by 2% to 5%: 60%. Against the updated brokers: upgraded: 15%; held within 1.5%: 45%; cut by more than 1.5%: 40%.
  • Net breadth: negative through the March report: 75%. Negative or neutral through August: 70%. Positive at some point in the second half of 2027: 35% to 40%.
  • Ratings and targets: these are more exposed than estimates. Targets rest on multiples well above the current one (Jefferies' $85 is 23x 2027 EPS), and the buy share has fallen for six months. A further fall in the buy share toward 60% in the first half of 2027 is more likely than a recovery.

Why not deeper cuts. Management flexes the cost line to deliver EPS; the buyback is contracted; the 2027 frame is already public; and the updated brokers are only 1% above us. A cut of more than 2% to 2027 needs something outside the base (currency, tariffs, or implantables staying negative in the United States through the first half).

Why not upgrades. There are no windfalls in 2027. The first half carries every dated headwind. In neither 2025 nor 2026 did the year finish above where consensus stood a year earlier without non-operating help. And when upside appears, management spends most of it. The upgrade drivers are specific: United States implantables positive by the August report (worth about 1% on 2028); the Q4 2026 reinvestment proving to be one-off (1.5% to 2% on 2027); a weaker dollar (1.25% of EPS for each 1%); a larger buyback (an extra $500 million a year adds about 1.3% to EPS each year).

Secondarily: 18 months to three years out (FY2029 and FY2030). FY2029 consensus is thin (six brokers for EPS at $4.80, four for a margin of 23.9%); we are about 2% below it. The central expectation is a cut of 2% to 4% as the 2028 base settles (55%), with 30% held and 15% upgraded. The upside tail is fattest at this horizon, though, for two reasons the brokers do not model. The first is the balance sheet: consensus has debt falling and the share count shrinking about 1% a year, which is cash simply building. Net debt reaches about 0.7x EBITDA by end-2028; returning to the 2025-2026 level of about 1.4x through buybacks would add 8% to 10% to EPS (second set). The second is the corneal cell therapy, with approval targeted for late 2028 and peak sales above $500 million per Redburn. The downside tail at this horizon is a large acquisition (2% to 4% dilution) and the 2028 margin.

What this means for the gap between our assessment and the estimates. The gap is small and slightly negative. The memo's test of "scope for forecasts to lead to upgrades over the coming 6 to 12 months" is not met, and current momentum in revisions is negative. Against that, the risk of a damaging downgrade cycle is low, and revisions should stop falling in substance by mid-2027.

Current multiple

D-0755
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C+5570th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: day to day the share price is mostly Alcon's own. Over the latest six months the peer basket explains 21% of its daily price variance and 79% is idiosyncratic; over 24 months the basket has explained about a fifth on average. The level of the multiple, however, is set in two layers. The sector sets the range (its median multiple is 17.1x, at the 2nd percentile of its own history), and company events set Alcon's place within that range (from an average premium of about 14% since the spin-off, to a discount of about 20% in October 2025, to roughly none today). The multiple is therefore company-driven in its timing and its relative position, and sector-driven in its ceiling. A re-rating that Alcon can earn by itself reaches about 19.5x to 20x. The guideline 22.51x needs the sector to re-rate as well. Two keys are needed, and Alcon holds one of them.

What the co-movement data show (daily returns, rolling windows of 126 trading days, against an equal-weighted basket of dashboard peers):

  • The latest window (21 May to 13 November 2026): correlation 0.46, beta 0.75, share of variance explained 21%. Yet the six-month return was identical, +15.8% for Alcon and +15.8% for the basket. The days were Alcon's own; the destination was the sector's.
  • The 24 month-end readings: the share explained ranges from 6% (October 2026) to 41% (July 2025), with a mean of 22% and a median of 20%.
  • The pattern in the readings: the link is tightest (35% to 41%) in windows that contain a market-wide shock (those ending April to July 2025), and weakest (10% to 14%, and 6% in October 2026) in windows dominated by Alcon's own results. The reading fell from 33% to 10% within a month as the window took in the Q1 2026 report, after which the shares fell 12% in two days and 19.7% in a month while the Swiss index rose 12.6% (Deutsche Bank, 12-May-26). Macro shocks pull Alcon into line with the sector; its own reports pull it out.
  • Which peers it moves with: the highest correlations are with Smith & Nephew (0.56) and Medacta (0.53), both rated F for earnings momentum. The lowest among the larger names are with the sector's winners: Demant (0.22) and Straumann (0.30). The market is trading Alcon with the medtech names whose estimates are falling, not with those enjoying a product cycle.

What the cross-section of the sector shows (dashboard, 14-Nov-26). All seven stocks rated F for earnings momentum, Alcon among them, sit at or below the 5th percentile of their own ten-year multiple range. The six rated A or B average the 30th percentile. In this sector today, falling estimates are enough to hold a stock at its floor multiple; rising estimates are needed to leave it, though they do not guarantee it (Straumann has A ratings for both momentum measures and sits at the 2nd percentile). And the large-cap peers whose estimates are stable or rising trade at 18.4x to 21.5x on the same 24-month-forward measure (Getinge 18.4x, Sonova 19.0x, Demant 19.7x, Straumann 21.5x). None trades at 22.5x.

What the history of the relative multiple shows. Alcon's premium to European medtech has averaged about 14% since the spin-off (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 2-Apr-25). It was about 6% in January 2025 and became a discount of about 20% in October 2025, after two guidance cuts in which the shares fell 23% in the year to date against 1% for the European healthcare index. That part was company-driven. Over the longer span, Alcon's forward multiple fell from an average of 27x in 2023 to about 18x in mid-2026 while its expected three-year EPS growth rose slightly, and Bernstein describes "a striking lack of multiple dispersion" across a sector being moved by the discount rate and by flows (4-Jun-26). That part was shared.

The guideline multiple, taken apart. Moving from 17.2x to 22.51x is a rise of 31%.

  • The company's part: restoring Alcon's average premium of about 14% to an unchanged sector median of 17.1x gives about 19.5x, a rise of 13% to 14%. This needs the named worry to clear and estimates to stop falling: on the first set's precedent, two consecutive quarters of positive United States implantables growth with stable share, which cannot be reported before August to November 2027. In our base (share stabilising, implantables at +1% to +3%) we would expect the premium to be restored only in part by March 2028, to perhaps 18x to 19.5x. A full restoration, or the 20x to 21.5x at which the sector's current winners trade, needs implantables growing at 3% or better and upgrades.
  • The sector's part: the remaining 15% needs the sector median to rise from 17.1x to about 19.7x. That depends on the discount rate (the United States ten-year yield is near 5%) and on rotation between sectors. It is undated and outside Alcon's control.

The same two layers set the downside. A sector capitulation, or a return to a 15% to 20% company discount, gives 14x to 14.5x, which is the first set's fair trough.

One further driver is neither company nor sector. Earnings are in dollars and the main listing is in francs. Redburn cut its target from CHF 86 to CHF 76 in October 2025 on its exchange-rate assumption alone. The model's return is in francs at a constant exchange rate.

What this means for the case. Delivering the EPS forecast is not sufficient for a re-rating: Alcon beat EPS expectations three times in 2026 and the shares fell after each. The multiple responds to the premium lens evidence and to the sector. For the memo's view of the fair multiple, the cross-check is this: 22.51x is close to Alcon's own five-year median (22.64x), but it is above what any upgraded large-cap peer commands in the present sector, and reaching it by 31 March 2028 requires both keys within about seven months of the earliest date on which the first can turn.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D4434th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the precedents say medtech consumables franchises outperform when (a) they own a product cycle where the patient pays and the technical lead converts into price, and (b) a worry clears while the multiple is at the bottom of its range; they underperform when growth slows and the multiple de-rates at the same time ("slow growth is punished twice"). Alcon today fits the set-up for (b) but not the trigger: the worry has re-worsened rather than cleared. It fits pattern (a) in contact lenses and Ocular Health but not in premium IOLs.

  • Worries clearing at low multiples: Morgan Stanley finds European medtech typically rebounds through multiple expansion in the year after a major sell-off. The sector digest's rule is "a results beat alone does not lift a share price: watch for the first beat-and-raise the share price rewards". Alcon has produced three EPS beats in 2026 (May, August, November), and each was followed by a lower share price, because the market's named worry (implantables share) did not clear.
  • Patient-paid premium: the hearing aid makers re-rated in 2026 (Sonova from about 16x to 20.2x) when artificial-intelligence platforms earned price at launch, but their technical lead lasts only 12 to 18 months, so the launch premium is rented. Alcon's own history is the strongest example: PanOptix took United States PC-IOL share from 35% (2019) to over 80% (2021), and the shares rose 32% in 2021. The reverse is happening now.
  • Slow growth punished twice: Carl Zeiss Meditec went from 37x to 16x over three years on China and share loss; Alcon's forward P/E fell from 43x (2020-21) to 27x (2023), about 18x (2025) and 17x now as growth slowed to about 5% and guidance was cut twice in 2025.
  • Operational outperformance in the sector: it comes from cost programmes landing with volume (Sonova's margin beat; Smith & Nephew's self-help under activist pressure), and from mechanical buybacks. Alcon has both but at modest intensity.

Fit: Alcon is the "known story after a fall" that the sector note says to buy, but only once the specific worry visibly clears. On precedent, the re-rating trigger is two consecutive quarters of positive implantables growth with stable share, which cannot happen before the Q1 2027 and Q2 2027 prints (May and August 2027).

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B+6690th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: to a fair trough of about 14x to 14.5x 24-month-forward earnings with no estimate cut, the shares would fall about 15%. With a plausible bear-case estimate cut of about 5% alongside the trough multiple, they would fall about 20% to 25%. In a severe case (13x on estimates 8% lower) they would fall about 30%. Against an upside of about 17% to 30% to a recovered 20x to 22x multiple, the asymmetry is roughly balanced, not compelling, until the implantables worry clears.

  • Today: $73.21 on the New York line (CHF 61.36 in Zurich, 13-Nov-26), 17.08x 24-month-forward consensus EPS of about $4.29; the five-year range is 14.95x to 30.86x (median 22.64x, 10th percentile 17.13x); ten-year "crash" band 20.3x and "conservative" band 22.9x on the dashboard's bands; 52-week low CHF 47.8.
  • Scenarios (24-month-forward EPS times multiple):
  • Trough multiple, no cut: 14.5x on $4.29 = about $62 (CHF about 52), -15%.
  • Bear: 14x on $4.08 (-5%: implantables negative through 2027, margin flat) = about $57 (CHF about 48, the 52-week low), -22%.
  • Severe: 13x on $3.95 (-8%: plus tariff escalation or a currency shock) = about $51 (CHF about 43), -30%.
  • Broker bears: Jefferies $55 (16x, Surgical growth of 5% a year to 2029); Morgan Stanley $60 (14.5x EBITDA); Morgan Stanley's down case of $45 if the pipeline fails.
  • Why 14x to 14.5x is a fair trough: it equals the five-year low, sits at the "crash" band of the memo's ladder, and matches de-rated peers (Carl Zeiss Meditec at 16x, Smith & Nephew at about 10x to 13.6x, Coloplast 17.9x) for a business still growing EPS about 10% a year with 1.4x leverage.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C4759th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market's narrative on Alcon today is "a quality franchise at its cheapest multiple, but losing the premium-lens battle; show me". The staircase offers one decisive, dated step (Vivity Pro, January 2027, with readable evidence by the Q1 and Q2 2027 prints in May and August 2027), framed by mostly incremental, already-expected steps (launches, savings, buybacks) and distant novel options (AURN001, adjustable lenses). Until implantables growth turns positive for at least two quarters, the narrative will remain negative regardless of EPS beats; the November 2026 sequence (an EPS beat, a guidance trim, shares -4%) confirms this. If Vivity Pro works, the narrative can flip quickly from "share donor" to "premium upgrade seller", and the stock's position at the 1st to 10th percentile of its multiple range gives that flip leverage. Model implication: the second set should treat H1 2027 implantables growth (and the United States PC-IOL share series) as the variable that decides which scenario the 18-month forecasts sit in. It should not treat equipment, contact lenses or Ocular Health as narrative movers.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–78

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the earlier answers are highly congruent in mechanism and direction, and moderately congruent in their numbers at the edges. The same four-part account recurs across some ninety answers without contradiction: an annuity (about 70% of sales) that compounds mechanically; one acute problem, concentrated at the premium lens edge; a margin governed by management's reinvestment choice more than by the cost structure; and a consensus that is fair for 2027. That agreement is itself evidence for predictability, because the forecast does not need any single contested answer to be right. Ten things jar. None moves base EPS by more than about 1%, but the first three change what the memo should say.

Why the congruence is real and not merely repeated. The answers were reached from independent directions and meet at the same numbers. The external forces give Alcon-weighted market growth of 4% to 5%; the competitive-advantage answers say Alcon's 29% share means it grows with the market plus mix; the mechanical-forces answer gives 4% to 5% revenue growth and 8% to 10% EPS growth without heroics; the record gives 6.0% a year of revenue growth and about 70bp a year of margin; the second set's model lands on 5% and 60 to 100bp. The management answers (capable, not outlier; guides realistically; flexes costs) explain the pattern the track-record answers find (revenue below consensus in seven of nine quarters, EPS above in seven). Nothing in the forecast contradicts a foundation answer.

The jars that change what the memo should say:

  1. Our position against consensus has been stated three ways that differ in sign. The first set says 0% to 1% below in 2027 and 2% to 3% below in 2028; the second set says 0.8% and 3.0% below; the model's record says 1.0% above and 1.1% below. Against the updated brokers we are 1.0% and 1.3% below. More important, the updated brokers' own 2028 EPS growth (the median of each broker's 2028 over its 2027) is +12.6%, almost exactly ours (+12.4%). The FactSet figure of +14.8% comes from setting a 2028 median of $4.40 against a 2027 median of $3.83 drawn from different and partly older samples. "The risk in consensus sits in 2028" is therefore weaker than the earlier sets say: it sits in a stale median and in about a third of brokers (four of twelve have 2028 growth of 13% or more, and the range runs from $4.02 to $4.51).
  2. The forecast describes stabilisation; the return of record assumes a full re-rating. The first set puts the fair multiple at 19x to 22x for a 10% to 12% compounder and says the re-rating trigger (two consecutive quarters of positive implantables growth) cannot occur before the May and August 2027 results. The second set has United States premium lens share stabilising at 60% to 65%, not returning to 70%. The model then applies 22.51x, above the first set's range, by 31 March 2028. This is the largest incongruence between our assessment and a figure the memo will quote (see D-07 and the judgement on the fourth set).
  3. The product-line base is not pinned, and it reverses one of the second set's corrections. The 2025 annual report puts FY2025 implantables at $1,782 million (cited in the China procurement research), not the second set's approximate $1,745 million. If consumables were about $3,030 million as the second set has them, FY2025 equipment was about $940 million, not $975 million, and FY2026 equipment lands at about $1,090 to $1,100 million (growth of +23%, +25% and +11% cc in the first three quarters and Bernstein's +6% for the fourth). That is close to the FactSet figure of $1,082 million which the second set called stale. On that base, consensus equipment for 2027 ($1,170 million) is growth of about 7%, which restores the first set's reading: consensus equipment is too high against management's "units stable, mix toward the standalone console", a console that sells for about $90,000 against about $175,000 for the combined system (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 11-Nov-26). About $60 to $70 million of 2027 consensus revenue (0.5%) is exposed on this line. It also means consensus implantables for FY2026 ($1,775 million) is a small decline, consistent with Bernstein's -0.5%, not growth of 1.7%. We have asked for the base in our research requests.

The jars that change dates or sizes within the base:

  1. China's second procurement round is earlier than the sets assume. The first set dates it to H2 2026 and the second set takes its revenue effect in H2 2027 and 2028. The research finds bids in September 2026 and implementation from January 2027 (China Daily, 10-Sep-26; Bernstein, 29-Oct-26), with price cuts of 30% to 50% (Morgan Stanley, 2-Sep-26) on the 7% to 8% of implantables sold in China ($125 to $143 million). That brings about 2 points of drag on reported implantables growth into H1 2027, the half in which the market will read that line as the test of Vivity Pro. We would now put 2027 implantables at about 0% (H1 -2% to -3%, H2 +1% to +3%) and not +1%. The effect is about -$18 million of revenue and -$0.02 of EPS: too small to change the group inputs, but large for the narrative (D-05).
  2. Medicare Part D coverage for Tryptyr is expected from mid-2027, not from January (management, January 2026; Bernstein, 12-Nov-26). Direct-to-consumer advertising waits for it, and Medicare prescriptions are filled at 14% against 29% for commercial plans (Phoenix Research, 20-Oct-26). The second set's $190 million for 2027 is reachable but falls later in the year.
  3. ENCELTO is sized at half of what management and three brokers expect. The second set has $50 to $60 million in 2027; Rothschild & Co Redburn, Bernstein and Jefferies carry 75 to 100bp of group growth ($80 to $100 million). At group level this offsets items 4 and 5. The combined effect of the three is to move 2027 growth from the first half to the second and from high-margin to low-margin lines.
  4. A third sampler arrives in 2027. Bausch + Lomb plans to launch its enVista Beyond extended-depth-of-focus lens in 2027 (10-Q, 28-Oct-26). The earlier sets frame the contest as Vivity Pro against Johnson & Johnson's PureSee, with competitor sampling fading after two quarters. A new entrant in the same lens class can restart it.
  5. The guidance record is slightly worse than the second set states. It records FY2024 as "met". The research shows the FY2024 sales guide was lowered during the year from +7% to +9% cc to +6% to +7%, and the top of the margin range trimmed (Jefferies, 13-Nov-24). In each of 2024, 2025 and 2026, therefore, the sales or margin guide was lowered or trimmed during the year. This matters for I-01.
  6. One tail risk is smaller than the second set implies, and another is not mentioned. The second set treats the Department of Justice's 2024 demand on servicing discounts as a live brake on bundling. The Department declined to intervene on 20 May 2026, which concluded the investigation (interim report, 10-Aug-26). Against that, an advisory opinion of the health department's inspector general (May 2026) says some bundles of equipment, software and supplies do not fit the established safe harbours.
  7. The level of United States premium lens share (66% or mid-50s) is a difference of measurement, not of direction. The 66% is a volume-weighted survey of high-volume clinics whose premium penetration is 35% against about 21% nationally (Jefferies, September 2026); the mid-50s is Redburn's calculated national figure; Bernstein has 67% for Q2 2026. The model works on implantables growth, so it is unaffected. For the memo: on Redburn's measure the first set's 65% invalidation line was crossed some time ago.

Two smaller points. (a) FY2026 core EPS appears as about $3.50 (first set), $3.47 (second set) and $3.55 (the model). We treat $3.47 to $3.50 as the honest range and put about 60% on a result inside the guided $3.49 to $3.55: the range was narrowed on 10 November with half the quarter known, and this management flexes costs to deliver EPS. The difference is 0.1 point of margin, below the precision of the inputs, so we have not changed them. (b) The second set's 2028 margin step (+100bp) cites Neurotech's dilution fading, while its cash inputs assume $350 to $400 million of new bolt-on acquisitions every year, each of which the first set says dilutes margin by 20 to 40bp for two years. If the deals are done, dilution does not fade; it rotates. We keep +100bp but regard it as the top of the plausible base, not its middle (D-02).

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

A6292nd percentile

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the narrative is comprehensive on one dynamic and partial or silent on most of the others. The premium lens share loss is known to everyone who owns or covers the stock, and it is priced: the shares stand at the 1st percentile of their ten-year multiple range and below at least one broker's stated downside value. The 2027 margin bridge, the equipment trough and the missed mid-term targets are well known to the sell-side. What is hardly articulated is the engine that produces the earnings (the annuity and the managed cost line), the tax step-up that hid part of 2026's operating growth, and the balance sheet's path toward net cash with the choice that forces. Equally unarticulated are three near-dated negatives: China's effect on the first-half implantables figure, the dependence of 2027 revenue growth on two launches, and the quality of core earnings. The market treats Alcon as a premium lens share story, not as a recurring-revenue compounder. The unpriced positives are slow and undated; the unpriced negatives are near and dated. The gap between our assessment and the narrative is therefore favourable over 18 months to three years, and not over the next six months.

A caution on the evidence. The research covers the documents of five houses (Bernstein, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Jefferies, Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley), several dozen surgeon and industry channel checks, and Pershing Square's reports. It contains no social-media commentary and very little direct investor commentary. What investors know is inferred here from price behaviour and from what brokers say of their clients. We have asked for research on this.

How well known each dynamic is:

Three points the Consumer should draw from this:

  1. The market's frame is the premium lens line, and the price shows it. The research written to test this concludes that the market treats Alcon as a premium lens story and not as a compounder. The price agrees. The shares fell after each of three EPS beats in 2026. They move most closely with the sector's downgraded names (D-07). The multiple is at the 1st percentile of ten years. Redburn's downside scenario of October 2025, now a year old (no Tryptyr revenue at all, implantables growth capped at 2% through 2028, no margin gain in Surgical) gave CHF 68, above today's CHF 61.36. The shares trade between the brokers' base cases (median target $86) and their bear cases (Jefferies $55; Morgan Stanley $45 to $60), nearer the latter. A narrative concentrated on about 4% to 5% of sales is pricing the other 95% at a floor multiple.
  2. "2026 was flattered" is half the story, and nobody tells the other half. The widely held view is that 2026's EPS growth owed about 4 points to the tariff refund net of reinvestment, the licensee milestone, currency and the buyback. That is right. What is not said is that the core tax rate rose from 17.4% to about 19.3%, taking 2 to 2.5 points off. Removing both, operating EPS growth in 2026 was about 10% to 11%, in a year when implantables went negative. That is the durable rate our base uses for 2027 to 2030. The market's doubt about the quality of 2026 is larger than the facts warrant.
  3. The unpriced negatives come first. In the first half of 2027 the narrative can still get worse without the facts changing: a second and third negative implantables figure (China included), first-half revenue below consensus, a formal guide that buries the old targets, and negative revisions that are partly mechanical. The risk is that the May 2027 report is read as a failure of Vivity Pro when it is a quarter of sampling and Chinese price cuts.

Secondarily: 18 months to three years out. The narrative says less the further out it looks, and what is missing is mostly favourable. The corneal cell therapy is called underappreciated by Pershing Square and sized by Redburn, but it is in few models. The adjustable lens with RxSight is treated as defensive. The margin reaching the mid-20s by about 2030 is in consensus only as a thin FY2029 figure. Net cash by about 2030 is in nobody's commentary. The longer-dated negatives that are equally absent are the weak record on acquired capital, the possibility that a third and fourth competitor keep premium lens share rotating, and customer consolidation eroding the bundle. On balance the gap between our assessment and the narrative widens in our favour with the horizon.

What would change the narrative, in order. (i) Johnson & Johnson's United States surgical growth slowing from +14%, visible in mid-January and mid-April 2027. (ii) United States implantables growth turning positive, at the earliest in the August 2027 report. (iii) Estimates ceasing to fall, so that the stock leaves the group at the floor multiple (D-07). (iv) The first result that the share price rewards, which the sector's own rule names as the signal to watch.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 15 November 2026, 11:39 to 12:02 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Alcon (ALC-CH): First set of questions - the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-14 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years). Money in USD and on the company's core (adjusted) basis unless stated; "cc" means constant currency; "bp" means basis points.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: the five judgements that matter most, and the data conventions

Source check done. The latest Earnings Review does cover the quarter to 30 September 2026: it states that Alcon reported Q3 2026 on 10 November 2026, and its figures match the FactSet block's Q3-FY2026 column. It is safe to rely on.

The five judgements that shape the model:

  1. Q3 2026 changed the case at its weakest point. Implantables (about 17% of sales and the highest-margin line) turned negative (-2% cc to $422 million, United States -2%, against consensus of +1.1%), and United States presbyopia-correcting lens (PC-IOL) share is back at about 66% (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 11-Nov-26), not the 70% to 74% that the 1-Oct-26 memo relied on. Two of the memo's three invalidation tests (share below 65%; implantables below zero for two quarters) are at the line or will probably be crossed with the Q4 print; the third (equipment below +15% cc for two quarters) is half crossed (Q3 +11%) and Q4 faces a +18% comparison.
  2. Outside implantables the engine is mechanical and intact. Consumables, contact lenses and Ocular Health (about 70% of sales) compound at mid-single to high-single digits on an installed base, wearer trade-up and pricing. 2026 core EPS growth of +12% to +14% cc is being delivered, but with help: the $58 million tariff refund, a currency tailwind of about 160bp on EPS growth, and a buyback.
  3. The margin path is the most important modelling variable, and it has been reset lower. Management pre-guided net 2027 core margin expansion of under 100bp (underlying leverage of 150 to 200bp less Neurotech dilution and the non-recurrence of the tariff refund). Q3 2026 core margin of 21.8% (+160bp) included the $58 million refund (about 210bp); without it, the margin was about 19.7%, down roughly 50bp year on year. The "mid-20s core margin by 2027" Capital Markets Day target is de facto abandoned. Consensus core EBIT margin: 21.1% (2026), 21.8% (2027), 23.1% (2028), 23.9% (2029, thin coverage).
  4. Sell-side revisions are negative but shallow (FY2027 EPS -2.6% over three months and -3% over twelve; buy share 65%, down 12 points in six months). There has been no capitulation. The risk in consensus sits in 2028 (EPS +14.8%, margin +130bp), not 2027 (+11%).
  5. The valuation already discounts a lot (24-month-forward P/E 17.1x, 10th percentile of five years, 1st percentile of ten), but the market's re-rating test is implantables, and the earliest evidence is Q1 to Q2 2027 (Vivity Pro launches January 2027).

Data conventions and contradictions the Consumer must handle:

  • Use the brokers' basis as the base year: FY2025 revenue $10,319 million, core EPS $3.07, EBITDA $2,649 million. FactSet's standardised revenue line runs $20 to $80 million above the company's figure (FY2025 $10,401 million; Q3 2026 $2,737 million against the company's $2,717 million). Do not mix the two.
  • The FactSet median FY2026 revenue of $10,931 million looks stale. It implies Q4 2026 revenue of about $2,747 million, only +1.7% year on year, which is inconsistent with the ~6% cc guide (about $11.0 billion reported once the +90bp currency effect is added). The named post-results brokers cluster at $11.0 to $11.1 billion. Model about $11.0 billion.
  • Core EPS by quarter: 2025 Q2 $0.76, Q3 $0.79, Q4 $0.78 (FY $3.07); 2026 Q1 $0.85 (Q2 Earnings Review; the Change Forces table shows $0.80), Q2 $0.84, Q3 $0.91, so 9M 2026 is $2.60. The FY2026 guide (+12% to +14% cc plus about +1.6 points of currency, about $3.49 to $3.55) implies a Q4 of roughly $0.89 to $0.95 against $0.78, with about $40 million of tariff-refund reinvestment landing in Q4.
  • FY2025 core operating margin: 19.8% (company); Bernstein uses 19.6%. Tariff refund: $58 million received in Q3 (often quoted as "$60 million"). US PC-IOL share series (sources disagree on timing, not on direction): over 80% before late 2024; 66% in Q1 2025; 70% to 74% after PanOptix Pro (to Q1 2026); 67% in Q2 2026 (Bernstein); about 66% late 2026 (Redburn).
  • One channel check claims premium IOL penetration fell from 35% to 25% in 2026. It is one practice's experience and conflicts with Morgan Stanley's market data (United States penetration up about 200bp in H1 2026) and Jefferies' surgeon survey (35% to 41%). Do not model it.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Alcon (ALC-CH; SIX and NYSE): the global eye care leader, reporting in USD in two segments: Surgical (implantables / intraocular lenses including presbyopia-correcting lenses, surgical consumables and packs, phaco and vitreoretinal equipment such as Unity) and Vision Care (contact lenses; Ocular Health, including Systane OTC drops and the prescription dry eye drug Tryptyr)

Request: T-P1

Peers: The Cooper Companies (CooperVision is the closest contact lens peer and carries a higher operating margin); Johnson & Johnson Vision (the main rival in intraocular lenses and contact lenses; a division of Johnson & Johnson); Bausch + Lomb (a surgical, contact lens and eye pharma peer with low surgical margins); Carl Zeiss Meditec (an ophthalmic equipment and lens peer running its ProfitUp cost programme); Sonova (a Swiss medtech with a franc cost base and a CHF 90 million productivity programme); Smith & Nephew (a medtech self-help and restructuring precedent); EssilorLuxottica (a vision care peer with scale manufacturing); RxSight (a premium lens entrant with a high gross margin and an operating loss) Inputs: What share of Alcon's cost of goods, SG&A and R&D is fixed versus variable, and what incremental (drop-through) margin has Alcon earned on extra revenue in each year from 2019 to 2026? Why did the core operating margin fall from 20.6% (2024) to 19.8% (2025) on 4% constant-currency growth (Alcon 2025 annual report), and how much was currency, tariffs, Aurion R&D and launch spending? How did margins move in the 2020 procedure collapse and the 2021 recovery? Alcon stated on 10-11 November 2026 that underlying operating leverage of 150 to 200 basis points in 2027 will be offset by Neurotech dilution and the non-recurrence of the 2026 tariff refund, limiting net core margin expansion to under 100 basis points: what are the size and duration of each offset, and how much operating loss does Neurotech's ENCELTO carry? The restructuring programme was expanded in November 2026 to $230 million of costs and $130 million of run-rate savings (from $150 million and $100 million): which cost lines, what phasing, and what have Alcon's previous programmes delivered against promise? Total operating expenses fell from 49% of revenue (2018) to 41% (2024) through shared services: how much further can shared services, the 4,100-strong sales force and artificial intelligence reduce SG&A? What are the relative margins of equipment, consumables, implantables, contact lenses and Ocular Health, and how will the 2027 shift to the lower-priced Unity CS console affect segment margin? How large are the 2027 cost pressures from tariffs (gross exposure stated at $80 to $100 million a year), memory chip and resin inflation, and the Swiss franc cost mismatch (a 1% dollar move equals $29 million of operating income per company disclosure), and how much can the move of equipment assembly into China and of lens production to tariff-neutral sites offset? What did the cost programmes at Carl Zeiss Meditec, Smith & Nephew, Sonova and Cooper deliver against plan? Focus: How much of the operating leverage Alcon describes for 2027 to 2029 actually reaches the core operating margin after acquisition dilution, launch spending, tariffs and input inflation, and how much of the cost base could realistically be cut if organic growth stays at the low end of the company's 6% to 8% target, as the chief executive said on 11 November 2026.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Johnson & Johnson Vision (the PureSee, Odyssey and Acuvue pricing benchmark); Bausch + Lomb (the enVista premium lenses, Miebo and Xiidra dry eye pricing); The Cooper Companies (contact lens pricing contribution guided at 1% to 3%); RxSight (light-adjustable lens pricing for surgeons and patients); Carl Zeiss Meditec (lens and equipment pricing under China procurement); STAAR Surgical (premium out-of-pocket implantable lens pricing); Sonova and Demant (patient-paid premium device pricing at launch, as an analogue) Operating conditions: an intense competitive share attack in premium intraocular lenses (Johnson & Johnson's PureSee United States launch in March 2026, free trials and sampling, discounts against Vivity); United States reimbursement cuts to surgeon fees (2026 and proposed 2027 Medicare physician fee schedule changes) and Germany's ban on private billing for premium lens procedures from July 2026; consumer affordability squeezes and recessions for out-of-pocket premium cataract lenses and contact lenses; government volume-based procurement in China; consolidation of buyers (private-equity-owned surgery centres, optical retail chains and club stores, online channels and private label); input cost and tariff inflation; currency moves Inputs: How much of Alcon's contact lens growth has come from price in each year since 2021 (Bernstein put it at about 4 points of 5% growth in Q2 2026), how did the two 2026 United States price increases stick with retailers and optometrists, and why is international pricing harder? Has Alcon cut list or net prices, or used rebates or bundles, on Vivity, PanOptix Pro or Clareon in response to PureSee (offered at about a 15% discount to Vivity in Europe per Rothschild & Co Redburn, October 2025) and Bausch + Lomb lenses (about $100 cheaper), and what are the trends in premium lens average selling prices in the United States and Europe? How did premium lens prices and Alcon's share behave in 2019 to 2021 (the PanOptix launch) and in 2024 to 2026 (Odyssey, enVista Envy, PureSee)? What will Vivity Pro (United States launch January 2027) be priced at against PureSee? How are Unity console prices, financing and bundled consumable pack prices holding up as surgery centres demand return-on-investment proof and buy items separately? How did Alcon pass on 2025 tariffs (the company says it offset an $80 million tariff headwind through pricing and efficiencies), and can it repeat that in 2027? What were the price cuts in China's procurement rounds for intraocular lenses (recent rounds 50% to 60% per Bernstein and Morgan Stanley), and what is expected in the 2026-2027 round? How do Tryptyr's net price and coverage evolve with Medicare Part D coverage in 2027, and how does Systane premium pricing hold against private label? How did premium lens conversion and contact lens trade-up behave in 2008 to 2009 and 2020? Focus: Whether Alcon's United States contact lens and OTC price increases are sustainable, and whether its premium intraocular lens prices can hold through 2027 to 2028 against PureSee and others or must fall (openly or through rebates and bundles) to defend share.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The case turns on whether Alcon's premium lens share loss and its Unity installed-base cycle are self-reinforcing or self-correcting, which decides whether implantables and consumables growth accelerate or stall over 2027 to 2029. Peers: Johnson & Johnson Vision (surgeon trialing and switching dynamics in premium lenses); Bausch + Lomb (enVista recovery after its 2025 recall); Carl Zeiss Meditec (installed-base and digital-workflow lock-in, and a de-rating precedent); RxSight (adjustable lens adoption loop); The Cooper Companies (contact lens capacity, share and scale loop); Sonova and Demant (two-year platform cycles and share mean reversion) Inputs: Does surgeon trialing of PureSee, enVista and light-adjustable lenses become habit (a reinforcing loss of Alcon premium share), or do Alcon's bundling, training and Vivity Pro (January 2027) reverse it, and how did past share swings end (Alcon's United States presbyopia-correcting share went from 55% in 2009 to 35% in 2019, over 80% in 2021, 66% in Q1 2025, 70% to 74% after PanOptix Pro, and about 66% in late 2026 per Bernstein and Rothschild & Co Redburn)? Does the Unity replacement cycle (an installed base of about 30,000 systems on a ten-year cycle; placements in 40% of United States teaching hospitals per Alcon, November 2026) reinforce consumables, lens pull-through and surgeon loyalty, and is there account-level evidence that a Unity placement raises Alcon's lens share? Or does the pulled-forward replacement wave create a post-cycle trough in equipment (balancing)? Does contact lens manufacturing capacity (DSM Flex lines, the Singapore and Grosswallstadt expansions) create a loop of share, scale, lower unit cost and more share? Do falling surgeon fees reinforce premium conversion (more premium offered) or balance it (less capacity, patients trading down to enhanced monofocals)? Does price-led competition at the premium edge, combined with Alcon's bundling and premium pricing, create customer resentment that accelerates switching? Does the buyback at a low multiple, combined with a low share price, reinforce EPS growth, and does a lower share price constrain acquisition-led innovation? Focus: Whether the current premium intraocular lens share loss is a reinforcing loop that persists beyond 2027 or a balancing one that Vivity Pro and the Unity installed base reverse, and whether the Unity cycle leaves a lasting consumables uplift or an equipment trough.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Consensus EPS growth for 2028 depends on a step-up in core margin plus several segment and below-operating-profit inputs (implantables recovery, equipment growth, acquisition dilution, tax, share count, currency) that the second set must quantify one by one. Peers: The Cooper Companies (a contact lens margin and tax benchmark); Bausch + Lomb (dry eye drug ramp benchmarks for Miebo and Xiidra); Carl Zeiss Meditec (equipment cycle and China procurement effects); Sonova (Swiss franc cost base and currency sensitivity); Johnson & Johnson Vision (premium lens share trajectory); Glaukos and Sight Sciences (MIGS growth and the Hydrus royalty) Inputs: United States presbyopia-correcting lens share and implantables growth in 2027 to 2028 after the Vivity Pro launch (implantables fell 2% in constant currency in Q3 2026 per Alcon); 2027 equipment revenue given management's statement (November 2026) that Unity unit volumes will be stable with a mix shift to the lower-priced Unity CS; consumables growth per installed console; the split between price and volume in contact lens growth; the Tryptyr sales ramp with Medicare Part D coverage in 2027 (Bernstein models about $110 million in 2026 and $400 million at peak by 2029); Neurotech ENCELTO revenue (Bernstein says it adds 75 to 100 basis points to group revenue once scaled) and its dilution to 2027 core earnings; the core gross margin path given tariffs, memory chip and resin inflation, and the new Singapore and Grosswallstadt lines; R&D held at the top of 7% to 9% of sales and direct-to-consumer launch spending; the phasing of the $130 million savings; the core effective tax rate (about 20% guided for 2026) and its outlook; net interest on 98% fixed-rate debt; the share count under the $1.5 billion three-year buyback net of equity-award dilution (grants exceeded vesting by 400,000 shares in Q1 2026); currency sensitivity (a 1% dollar move equals $52 million of sales and $29 million of operating income per company disclosure); and the reconciliation from core to IFRS EPS (amortisation of acquired intangibles, restructuring, impairments). Focus: Which inputs make the gap between management's framework for 2027 (net core margin expansion under 100 basis points; growth at the low end of 6% to 8%) and consensus EPS for 2028, and how far apart management, the sell-side and the evidence are on implantables, equipment, margin, tax and share count.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 15 November 2026, 20:16 to 20:32 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Alcon (ALC-CH): Second set of questions - the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years

Written 2026-11-15 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in USD millions and on the company's core (adjusted) basis unless stated; "cc" means constant currency; "bp" means basis points. "Our base" means the forecasts recorded in forecast-inputs.md.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: the base case on one page, the conventions, and where this set disagrees with the first

The base case in one line. Alcon compounds core EPS at about 10% to 11% a year from 2026 to 2030 ($3.47, $3.80, $4.27, $4.71, $5.18) on revenue growth of about 5% a year and core operating margin gains of about 60 to 100bp a year (21.0%, 21.6%, 22.6%, 23.3%, 23.9%), with a net share count reduction of about 1% a year. That is Alcon repeating its own 2019 to 2024 record (about +70bp of margin a year, EPS +11% a year from 2022 to 2025), not accelerating beyond it.

Against consensus (FactSet median, 14-Nov-26): FY2026 in line on revenue (our $11,033 million against a stale median of $10,931 million but in line with the named post-results brokers at $11.0 to $11.1 billion) and a touch below on EPS ($3.47 against the guide's low end of $3.49 and a median of $3.45). FY2027 in line on revenue level ($11,585 million against $11,548 million) and about 1% below on EPS ($3.80 against $3.83). FY2028 in line on revenue ($12,199 million against $12,200 million) and about 3% below on EPS ($4.27 against $4.40), because we have the core margin 50bp below consensus (22.6% against 23.1%). FY2029 about 2% below on EPS ($4.71 against $4.80, six brokers). The gap with consensus is a margin gap in 2028, not a revenue gap.

The single most important modelling judgement: the 2027 margin is decided less by "operating leverage" than by two policy choices management controls: how much of the 150 to 200bp of underlying leverage it reinvests, and whether the $40 million of tariff-refund money it reinvested in Q4 2026 becomes a recurring cost. Each of those is worth 20 to 40bp of 2027 margin, as much as the whole implantables recovery.

Conventions the Consumer must hold to:

  • Base year on the company's (brokers') basis: FY2025 revenue $10,319 million, core EBIT $2,039 million (19.8%), core EPS $3.07. FactSet's standardised revenue runs $20 to $80 million a year above the company's figure (FY2025 $10,401 million). Growth rates in our inputs apply to either basis; levels do not.
  • Company quarterly revenue (USD million): Q1 2025 about 2,453 (derived), Q2 2,577, Q3 2,589, Q4 about 2,700; Q1 2026 2,685 to 2,690, Q2 2,782, Q3 2,717. 9M 2026 is $8,184 million (+7.4% reported).
  • Core EBIT margin by quarter, 2026: Q1 21.2%, Q2 20.6%, Q3 21.8% (including the $58 million tariff refund, about 210bp); 9M 21.2%, core EBIT about $1,735 million.
  • Our "EBITDA" is core EBITDA: core EBIT plus depreciation and software amortisation, excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles, at about 5.5% of sales ($600 million in 2026). It is not FactSet's "operating EBITDA" history line, which runs on a different basis (FactSet shows Q4 2025 at 27.3% and Q2 2026 at 11.9% because of the PowerVision impairment). Do not compare our margins with that line.

Where this set disagrees with the first set's answers, and why (each is used in the model):

  1. The consensus equipment forecast is not "+8% in 2027" in any meaningful sense. The first set (C-67, C-85) called consensus equipment growth of +8% "high". That growth rate is computed off a stale FY2026 consensus of $1,082 million, which implies Q4 2026 equipment of about $281 million, roughly -10% year on year after +11% in Q3. On a realistic FY2026 of about $1,130 million, the FY2027 consensus level of $1,170 million is only about +3%. Our +1% is below it, but the gap is about $25 million of revenue, not $90 million. The same stale-base artefact inflates every consensus growth rate for FY2027 computed from the $10,931 million revenue median; compare levels, not growth rates.
  2. FY2026 core EPS lands at $3.47, not about $3.50. The guide's low end ($3.49) needs a Q4 core EPS of $0.89 against $0.78, which in turn needs a Q4 core margin of about 20.8% while about $40 million (about 140bp of Q4 sales) of refund money is being spent, and a core tax rate that has risen from 17.4% (2025) to 19% to 20% (Pillar 2). We put Q4 at about 20.4% and EPS a cent or two below the low end. This matters for the Consumer because every 2027 growth rate in broker notes is struck off a 2026 base that may be 1% too high.
  3. The tax rate is not "roughly neutral" in 2026 (C-49). The core effective tax rate was 17.4% in 2025 and is guided at 19% to 20% for 2026 because of Pillar 2 (Q3 2026 earnings presentation). That is a headwind of about 3% to 2026 EPS growth, absorbed inside the guide; from 2027 tax is a small, stable drag (about -0.8% in 2027 as it moves to 20%).
  4. The share count guide has moved. The company now guides about 487 million weighted diluted shares for 2026 (November 2026), down from 492 million (May 2026). The first set used 492 million.
  5. Amortisation of acquired intangibles is about $0.5 billion a year, not about $0.8 billion (C-54). It was $388 million in 9M 2026 (Q3 2026 press release). The core-to-IFRS gap is still large, but it is shrinking.
  6. United States presbyopia-correcting lens share is less certain than "about 66%". The feedback-loop research for this set reads the same Rothschild & Co Redburn note (11-Nov-26) as showing share "in the mid-50s by late 2026", and Redburn's own model (2-Apr-25) has share falling from 75% (2024) to 60% (2027). We treat the current level as somewhere between the high 50s and 66%, and model about 60% to 65% through 2027, stabilising rather than recovering.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Basis of every input. Revenue growth is reported (USD) growth on the company's basis (FY2025 $10,319 million). FactSet's standardised revenue runs $20 to $80 million a year above the company's (FY2025 $10,401 million); applied to that base, our growth rates give levels about 0.8% higher than ours. "EBITDA" is core EBITDA: core operating income (which excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles, restructuring, impairments and other items above a $10 million threshold) plus depreciation and software amortisation. It is not FactSet's "operating EBITDA" history line. Core EBIT margin is therefore EBITDA margin less about 5.5 points: 21.0%, 21.6%, 22.6%, 23.3%, 23.9%. The tax rate is the core effective rate. The share-count change line is shares issued to employees under equity plans (net of shares bought back is entered separately); grants exceeded vesting by 0.4 million shares in Q1 2026 alone. Lease payments are an estimate: the files do not disclose them; FactSet's net debt including leases ($3.53 billion) against the company's measure excluding them ($3.25 billion at 30 June 2026) and the guided lease interest within non-operating expenses point to a lease liability of a few hundred million dollars and payments of about $100 million a year. Acquisitions: 2026 is Neurotech ($550 million upfront, closed 20 October 2026) plus small items; 2027 to 2030 are an assumption of continued bolt-ons at the bottom of the $300 to $600 million a year that the company's record suggests, including milestones (RxSight up to $140 million; Neurotech).

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Alcon reports two segments (Surgical; Vision Care) with segment contribution as the measure, and five product lines. Segment margins are our estimates, anchored on FY2025 segment contribution (Surgical about $1.5 billion, about 26% of $5,751 million; Vision Care $981 million, about 21.5% of $4,568 million; Q3 2026 Surgical 25.7% and Vision Care 25.8%, Bernstein 10-Nov-26). FY2025 product-line revenue is approximate (derived from quarterly disclosures).

The allocation of ENCELTO between segments is not disclosed; we place it in Ocular Health (about $50 to $60 million in 2027, rising toward 75 to 100bp of group revenue once scaled per Jefferies and Bernstein). Surgical margin is held back in 2027 by implantables (the highest-contribution line) at +1% and helped slightly by the lower equipment mix; Vision Care margin rises as the Singapore and Grosswallstadt contact lens lines fill (24 to 36 months) and Ocular Health mix improves, less Tryptyr launch spending and Neurotech dilution.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue (about +5% a year): global cataract procedures about 3% (United States 0% to 2% in 2027; surgeon capacity is the constraint); United States presbyopia-correcting IOL share stabilising at about 60% to 65% after Vivity Pro (United States launch January 2027), not returning to 70%+; implantables +1% in 2027 (H1 about -1%, H2 about +3% against a -2% comparison) and +3% a year after, net of premium net price -1% to -2% and the 2026-27 China procurement round (30% to 40% price headwind expected on IOLs per Morgan Stanley); consumables +5% on the installed base (about 40% of consumables tied to Alcon hardware); equipment +1% in 2027 (units stable, mix to the lower-priced Unity CS, H1 comparisons of +23% and +25% cc) and +3% a year after; contact lenses +5% in 2027 (one United States price increase rather than the two of H1 2026; international growth 1% to 2%) and +5.5% in 2028 to 2029 on mix; Ocular Health about +8% organic in 2027 (Tryptyr from about $110 million to about $190 million with Medicare Part D coverage, below Bernstein's $225 million because of tolerability reports) plus ENCELTO. Currency is held at zero from 2027.
  • Quarterly shape: revenue growth dips in H1 2027 (about +4.2% and +4.3%) on equipment comparisons and two more quarters of competitor IOL sampling, and recovers in H2 2027 (about +5.6% and +5.9%) as equipment comparisons ease and implantables lap -2%.
  • Margin: underlying operating leverage of about 150bp in 2027 (the low end of management's 150 to 200bp), less the non-recurrence of the $58 million tariff refund (50 to 55bp) and a 2026 licensee milestone (10 to 15bp), Neurotech dilution (about 20bp), memory chip and resin inflation (about 20bp) and currency (about 10bp), partly offset by the non-recurrence of about 70% of the roughly $40 million of refund money reinvested in Q4 2026 (about +15 to +20bp): net +60bp. 2028 +100bp as Neurotech dilution fades, implantables mix stops hurting and leverage at 5.3% growth works, less AURN001 pre-launch spending and bolt-on dilution; 2029 +70bp and 2030 +60bp. About $80 million of incremental restructuring savings land in 2027, mostly spent offsetting inflation and tariffs; no new programme is assumed.
  • Quarterly margin shape: Q3 2027 core margin is down about 110bp year on year because Q3 2026 included the $58 million refund (about 210bp of that quarter); Q4 2027 is up about 180bp because Q4 2026 carried about $40 million of reinvestment. Both are comparison effects. The Q4 2025 core margin is not pinned in the files (one table shows 19.0%), so the Q4 2026 and Q4 2027 margins carry low confidence.
  • Below the line: net financial items within the guided $210 to $230 million for 2026, drifting down as cash builds (98% fixed-rate debt); core tax 19% to 20% in 2026 (17.4% in 2025; Pillar 2) and 20% thereafter; weighted diluted shares about 487 million (2026, guided), 482, 478, 474 and 467 million.
  • Cash: capex including intangibles about 6% of sales (Singapore, Grosswallstadt, Unity); working capital held at about 33% of revenue (32.6% to 34.8% since 2024; 33.2% at Q3 2026); buybacks of $500 million a year to complete the $1.5 billion programme (about $1.0 billion remaining) by end-2028, then $800 to $900 million a year as net debt falls below 0.5x EBITDA; dividends growing about 10% a year (payout about 10% of core EPS).

The formal FY2027 guide comes with the FY2026 results (2 March 2027 per FactSet). We expect it to be set at or slightly below our base.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue: consensus (brokers' basis) uses the company's reported net sales (FY2025 $10,319 million); FactSet's standardised history line is $20 to $80 million a year higher (FY2025 $10,401 million; Q3 2026 $2,737 million against the company's $2,717 million). The FactSet FY2026 median ($10,931 million) is stale: it implies Q4 2026 of about $2,750 million (+2% year on year); the named post-results brokers sit at $10.96 to $11.12 billion. Consensus growth rates for FY2027 computed off the stale median overstate consensus growth (for example, equipment "+8%" is about +3% on a realistic FY2026 base); compare levels.
  • Operating profit and EBITDA: consensus EBIT is core operating income. Core operating income = IFRS operating income plus amortisation of acquired intangibles (about $0.5 billion a year; $388 million in 9M 2026), restructuring (about $150 million in 2026 under the $230 million programme), impairments ($505 million gross on PowerVision in Q2 2026), and other items (transaction costs, legal, fair-value changes) above a $10 million threshold; 9M 2026 adjustments totalled about $1.1 billion. Consensus EBITDA (brokers' basis, 25.7% in FY2025) adds back depreciation and software amortisation to core EBIT; FactSet's "operating EBITDA" history (25.9% in FY2025; 11.9% in Q2 2026) is a different, IFRS-based line and must not be compared with our inputs.
  • EPS: consensus EPS is core diluted EPS (FY2025 $3.07). IFRS basic EPS was $1.99 in FY2025 and about $0.93 in 9M 2026; we estimate about $1.45 for FY2026, about $2.80 for FY2027 and about $3.40 for FY2028 (the impairment drops out; amortisation is flat while earnings grow). FactSet's "diluted before unusual items" ($1.89 in FY2025) is a third basis. The core tax rate (19% to 20%) differs from the reported rate (15.3% in 2025) because of deferred-tax effects on amortisation and impairments.
  • Free cash flow: our cross-check deducts capex including intangibles and lease payments from operating cash flow; FactSet's definition deducts only fixed-asset capex (FY2025 $1,562 million against about $1,535 million on ours); the company reported $1.70 billion for FY2025 on its own definition.
  • Net debt: FactSet includes leases ($3.53 billion at Q3 2026); the company's measure excludes them ($3.25 billion at 30 June 2026).

Earnings per share 3.47 3.80 4.27 4.71 5.18
